# Brainstorm: realism additions to players, teams, tactics, and the match engine

## The Brainstorm

Session 11 settled the realism test and how fast it runs. The yardstick is a model fitted on real results: each team has attack and defence numbers that change through a season, with home advantage and a small adjustment per league, a draw correction, five seasons of football-data.co.uk, and a long history for the slow parts. It judges results, the table, and each team's counts per gap in team strength. A band passes only when its whole uncertainty range, the real value's uncertainty included, sits inside a margin worked out per band; one simultaneous test over all bands keeps a sound engine passing 97 percent of change runs, and rare bands use exact bounds and a minimum exposure. A change run plays at least 12,000 matches within ten minutes against a stated intent, over many generated leagues, and gives every band a verdict against the old engine: better, worse, equivalent, or not sure. Every tactic setting is tested through covering arrays of both teams, only the array cells stop early, and an exploit search runs before release. Tuning fits each value with its own data bottom-up, then searches the rest on the full engine with a loss that is zero inside a band and hard rules as limits; the emulator only screens and triages, inside a recorded domain, and must repay its cost. For speed, result-identical code speed-ups go first behind a replay check, then a harness that keeps a compact row per match, resumes by fixture key, and checks every match with a running checker. An older outside report agreed with this direction and found four real defects in today's harness code. The coherence pass then cut the in-match forks from session 7, so every test plays full matches, and kept early stopping to the tactics-array cells.

Session 10 turned to the realism test itself: the harness, the bands, the tests, the statistical model behind them, and fast iteration, with the new module architecture in view. Six researchers read the code and the literature. Today sixteen bands pass on fixed ranges, the sampling error decides nothing, and hand tuning breaks one band while fixing another. You said all four failures matter: bands that fight, a slow verdict, invisible noise, and play that looks wrong. You started with the yardstick and the pass rule. A model fitted on five real seasons judges results and the table, measured ranges judge the rest, and both compare per gap in team strength. A band passes by an equivalence test inside a margin set as a share of the real spread, every band can block, and the developer decides a not-sure result. A verdict on every band must arrive in about five minutes, so the harness needs about twelve times today throughput, a performance project of its own. The first harness version may keep the fixed ranges, a risk you accept. Still open: judging one change, realism beyond averages, and fast tuning, where the rule that bands are never tuning targets meets the emulator.

You started from one thought. The in-progress plans for Touchline build a lawful, tactical, calibrated match, and you wanted to know what they still miss on realism. Six sessions later, the answer is a whole product. Touchline is a football-management game, and the match engine is its first component. The engine workflow keeps its charter and its sixteen slices. The realism work runs as a separate programme after it, measured against the shipped engine with a performance budget of its own.

What we believe now, area by area:

- **The player.** A player becomes a person. The single position code becomes a competence rating per position, and each foot gets its own rating. Players gain new performance attributes, a personality with hidden traits, and habits that training can change. Under pressure or low mood, a strong habit can override an instruction, and the manager can see why.
- **The club.** On the pitch, a club's style comes from its squad and its tactic. Club identity acts off the pitch: it shapes who the club hires and buys, and what the board and fans expect, blended with the owner type. A club also has a home ground, a reputation, and a team mood that personality shapes.
- **Tactics.** Each team has an in-possession shape and an out-of-possession shape, set-piece routines, and live changes that wait for the next stoppage. Both managers can make those changes.
- **The match.** The engine resolves every kind of action from the situation first and the player second. It uses the height the ball already has for play in the air. It has a full referee with a video assistant, and injuries have a severity. The player's own match plays in real time or as key moments, and the other matches of the day run beside it.
- **The realism test.** The test measures against real match data, and its bands are far tighter than the plan's first guess. Where the game departs from real data on purpose, the band has a real target and a design target. People watch matches and seasons at milestones to review them. The test keeps its StatsBomb and Transfermarkt sources: you accept the licence risk, because the game ships none of the data. Cups get one band of their own, the upset rate by the gap in divisions.
- **The season.** The season layer mirrors the real European structure. It adds careers with age curves, a market that values players from visible data only, and a club business with a board and an owner who can sack the manager. The fast model that runs the other leagues is set out under engine structure. A new save may take a few minutes to create. Money is kept in euros inside the game, and the player chooses the currency on screen.
- **The staff.** Staff are people who also carry a rating, and each club sees that rating through its own judgment. Every role a real club employs is in the game, from coaches and scouts to the groundsman, the psychologist, and the loan manager. Each one has attributes, a personality, specialisms, a playing idea, a contract, and a market. Numbers and quality differ by league and tier. Staff are full people only in the active leagues, and the player chooses per department whether to hire directly or to delegate. Staff speak through reports, a word during the match, and meetings. Their advice can be wrong through noise, a personal leaning, an agenda, or a blind spot. Overruling them has a cost, and the game remembers who was right. Staff follow a manager through an inner circle and their own loyalty, other clubs can poach them for a fee, and they have careers: retired players become coaches, and coaches become managers. Staff know some places better than others, form bonds with players, tire under too much work, and need facilities the club pays for. On the pitch, staff decide how fast the squad learns set-piece routines and tactics, how the team fades late in a match, and how well an opposition plan lands. Session 8 settled their attributes: staff and managers share one list of 32 attributes, and each role reads a weighted part of it, so suitability for any role is computed; staff also carry the players' twelve traits, five traits of their own, three hidden values led by consistency, a languages list, and up to ten leaning tags.
- **Contracts and the dressing room.** A contract is negotiated between the agent, the director of football, the manager, and the player's family, and the player weighs the role, the project, and life off the pitch as well as money. Every agent is a person, but only a few large agencies hold real power. A contract carries money, bonuses, clauses, and promises; most promises are verbal and move trust, and a few are written. The years left on a contract lower the price, and a release clause caps it. The dressing room has standing, groups, a culture, an icon, and a view of the manager. Unrest spreads through leaders and groups, escalates from a private word to a refusal to play, and has a small effect on the pitch. The manager disciplines within each country's real rules, with warnings, fines, dropping, training apart, and public pressure, and strong standing at the club stretches those limits. A manager who crosses the line faces the board and a tribunal that can award anything up to a free exit.
- **Supporters and the press.** Supporters are groups with their own expectations, and the club's fan mood is the balance between them. Fans react to the club's identity, and they hold power through the board, real ownership models, money, and the manager directly. The crowd moves the referee, nervous players, and the home team's late belief. Rivalries come from the world data, geography, and events in the save. The manager attends every press conference, can delegate one, and picks the players where a competition requires them, as UEFA does. Stories build over weeks from real events and invented rumours. Journalists are people, and the manager can give exclusives, leak, freeze someone out, and learn who is reliable. How media pressure reaches the manager's job depends on the owner.
- **Where new players come from.** Academies follow each country's real structure, and the full player model starts at the scholarship age, about 16; younger players form a rough pool that clubs learn about slowly through staff, time, tests, and other clubs' interest. A young player's future is a range that narrows, with no fixed ceiling. Clubs over-rate early-born players, as real academies do, and good staff can correct it. Players also arrive as late bloomers, through the lower leagues, and from beyond UEFA. The generator copies measured patterns: birth quarters, late maturers, growth over decades, each nation's output, and dual eligibility. A player's style comes from where the player develops, not from nationality. B teams and squad registration follow real rules by country and competition. The world may drift over a long save, and the season test keeps it inside real ranges.
- **International football.** The calendar, national teams, and players on duty are in the game; managing a nation is a later addition. Call-ups follow club form, the national coach's taste, loyalty, and the nation's depth. The manager can play a player into a call-up or ask for rest, players choose between nations and can retire from duty, and duty brings fatigue, injuries with compensation, mood, and changes in value and fame.
- **The manager's week.** Team talks before, during, and after the match move mood and focus, and they depend on reading the room, on not repeating one approach, on words to single players, and on the manager's standing. Delegation works the same way in every area. Information arrives in a daily inbox the player can filter, periodic reports from delegated work, and urgent escalations from staff. Reasons behind events show in a mixed way. Time is limited by the structure of the week: training slots, one briefing per match, team talks, press conferences, a few one-to-one talks, a staff meeting, and trips, with no hour budget; a task done personally uses the manager's own attributes and builds bonds faster. Clubs can fall into crisis, and the realism test reaches off the pitch with bands for measured facts and design targets for the rest.
- **The data model.** Every attribute keeps a named job in the engine. An action runs in stages, and each stage blends a main attribute with a few supporting ones, weighted from real data where it exists. Each overlapping pair has its own split, and pace is held back three ways. Attributes are stored in tenths behind the familiar 1-to-20 display. A convex curve makes the top of the scale count more; its strength is set so a top player looks better in about two of three runs of five matches, and the same convexity prices development and the market. The world's spread of ability is wide on purpose. One effective-ability layer moves a player within bounds through body, mind, familiarity, and surroundings, so an 18 has poor patches and hot streaks but never becomes poor or unstoppable. Consistency and injury proneness are hidden and shown in words, and personality traits use the same 1-to-20 scale. A player's grasp of each tactic is personal, and the team's familiarity is computed from the players on the pitch. About 40 roles each set where a player stands, what the player prefers, and how teammates use the player, and a duty scales the role. A sensitivity test of its own changes one attribute, setting, or role at a time and checks that only the related statistics move. Team numbers act on actions that need several players, such as pressing chains, and club identity acts only off the pitch, through hiring and through the pressure of the board and the fans. Every rating in the game, for staff, managers, referees, and agents too, uses the same scale. Height gives reach at a small cost in turning, age slows recovery, and a player new to a country adapts over months. A relationship between two people keeps on-pitch understanding apart from personal liking.
- **Action resolution.** Each contact, meaning a tackle, a first touch, a keeper's handling, a strike, a pass, or an interception, draws from a chance or a spread built from the situation and the attributes, and physics carries the ball between contacts. A strike takes two draws, quality first and then error. A keeper's reach is physics and the handling a draw, and a penalty adds a guessing game. A duel runs in two steps and ends four ways. Errors come from three sources, the tails of the draws, concentration lapses, and a few named error events, and each source has a fixed share of the error band. The checks judge shots with an xG model fitted on real data, and band the shot split, pass curves, duels, Opta's errors leading to goals, and season variance with real and design targets.
- **Calibration.** Targets come from five recent seasons of real data (2021-22 to 2025-26) in three layers: each league with data, each tier, and banding groups for the other leagues. A division's style emerges from its players, not from settings by hand. Each band's tolerance is the real spread widened by sampling noise. The check compares every scoreline and whole distributions, not only averages, and bands the league table in full. Every report is stored with its version and compared with the last. Each change runs about 1,000 matches. Each milestone runs the touched parts of the heavy checks, and a full release run finishes overnight on a Mac mini and a VPS.
- **Tactical counterplay.** Costs, counters, and squad fit emerge from play: a high line is exposed because a slow defender loses the race, and a press tires the players who run it. There is no counter table and no fit number. Rest defence follows the duties, and the gaps between the lines emerge from where roles stand. A round-robin of eight named styles with identical squads and a grid over the settings prove that every style has a clear weakness. AI managers read the same noisy staff advice as the human, remember what worked, and carry a tactical fingerprint that analysts can reveal. Paired squads prove that fit matters. Beyond the brief, the AI learns during a match and an exploit hunt runs before each release, players manage games themselves with the manager's instructions on top, the manager can shout from the touchline, and conditions change which tactic works.
- **Engine structure.** The full engine runs every match in the active leagues, and a fast model runs the rest. The fast model is refitted from the full engine after each change, and it must match the full engine both in season bands and per pairing of team strengths on a fixed batch. A written state machine names every phase's entry and exit, and a check confirms where players stand at each restart. Grounds differ in size within the laws. Added time prices each kind of event, including video reviews. A match replays identically on every supported machine. A skipped match finishes on the fast model from its current state, background matches run first and reveal their events in step with the player's match, players near the ball think on every tick while the others think at a human rate, and a mid-match save finishes on the engine version it started with.
- **Player decisions and off-ball movement.** Each player works from a picture of the pitch that timed scans refresh, holding where others are heading and what team-mates call. A choice takes decision time and an action takes commit time, and pressure arriving inside either hurries or blocks it. The carrier chooses from the whole catalogue of 53 real actions plus game extras, scored as expected value with weights fitted to real choices; a poor decision-maker misjudges values and picks loosely. Without the ball, players move from an anchor through role patterns and an off-ball utility, and share space and defensive jobs by claims and calls, inside the team's marking system and counter-press window. Positional instructions are limits, choice instructions are weights followed as faithfully as the player's decisions and teamwork allow, and traits show as repeated choices and styles. Bands check shape, crowding, runs, pressure, handoffs, frozen players, and passes through crowds.
- **Output and presentation.** The engine writes a log of true causes, and every figure, visualisation, commentary line, rating, and staff sentence comes from the one match record, is recomputed by a test, and traces back to its moments. Figures are always true; staff readings and advice can be wrong by staff quality, and better analysts unlock more depth. Commentary is text with sound, from AI-written, reviewed banks that know player history, the season, the match's own stories, and the causes. Ratings value every action and off-ball contribution, fairly for each role. Highlights rank by win-probability swing, moments the manager can act on, and story. A 180-item catalogue of statistics and visualisations fills designed, customisable screens, with a provider-style xG, one definition per metric, and plain names. Staff explain live, at half-time, after the match, and across matches. The playtest asks testers why a match was won or lost and checks their answers against the true causes.
- **In-game management.** Substitutions and formation changes wait for a stoppage, while shouts and instructions start at once and spread through the players over time. The manager has team instructions, opposition targets, player instructions, and prepared game-state plans, and sees each player's state, the analysts' read of the opponent, what each change did, and the assistant's costed proposals. The match pauses freely or on chosen events. Each competition has its own dated rule pack, concussion substitutes included where allowed, and the manager decides on a head knock after the medics advise. The AI manager has the same rules, information, and reaction time, and uses every tool as well as its skill allows. Every change has a cost, forked matches with paired seeds band its effect against real game-state data, and the exploit hunt tries sequences of changes. After a skip, the manager's plans and triggers apply.
- **Set pieces.** The manager builds routines from a library, an editor, and the set-piece coach's proposals, names takers per kind and side, and sets defensive jobs within a zonal, man-marking, or mixed system. First contact comes from the delivery's flight, each player's run, height, and jumping, and a heading duel, with blocks and holding the referee may punish. Throw-ins include long-throw, retention, and quick throws. Routines need drilling, a squad holds only a few well, and opponents learn a favourite routine. Each routine's rates are banded, the set-piece goal share has a band per league that may drift as the world copies what works, penalties are banded per league with a shoot-out pressure curve, and a coach's effect stays inside the real spread.
- **Fatigue, injuries, and congestion.** A player tires through a short sprint reserve, a slow reserve, and mental fatigue, and paces himself when low, but late tiredness stays as modest as real data shows. Between matches, condition recovers over about three days while an accumulated load, seen only through the staff, raises injury risk. Injuries come from a hazard on each tackle, sprint, collision, landing, and turn, with real types, layoffs, and recurrence, plus training injuries, at real frequency. Returns pass through phases, knocks carry over and can be hidden, and history raises risk by site. Congestion costs mostly injury risk, as in real football, and rotation is also pushed by squad morale, sharpness, the calendar, and staff advice. Training follows a daily rhythm and a preseason base, sharpness comes from matches by level and match-simulation sessions, travel adds jet lag, and illness can spread through a squad. Serious injuries offer treatment choices in which the player and agent have a say, and a return affects confidence and caution. Medicals and market values read injury history, and an injury crisis reaches the board, the players, and the press. Background leagues carry the same state. Season bands check counts, burden, types, recurrence, and the congestion effect on full-engine seasons.
- **Long-term integration.** Each match feeds the wider game: young players grow from training, minutes at the right challenge, and good performances; form runs at two speeds; reputation and value follow level-weighted ratings and big stages; and the board, the fans, and income read results against expectation. Loops are dampened by modest, banded mood, complacency, opponents who lift, real league rules, wage pressure, ageing, poaching, and rivals who react, plus a soft pull in AI choices. Every match shows its stakes. A multi-season check bands title turnover, fortune churn, and promotion and relegation rates, and guards stop long-save drift.
- **Match preparation.** Knowing the opponent carries preparation. Separate analyst attributes set how well the report predicts the line-up, finds habits, and states its confidence, and analyst workload sets when it arrives. The staff prepare the plan, the training week, and the briefing by default, and the manager edits. Rehearsal gives a grasp of this match's instructions that fades after it, and the briefing moves how faithfully players follow the targets, what each knows of his opponent, and mood. Preparation helps only where the squad fits the plan, costs staff workload and training time, and has a wide band set after playtests. Surprises come from real change, adaptability, disguise, and leaks, AI managers read the manager's whole fingerprint, and the post-match report shows which prepared points worked.
- **Challenge and learning.** The game serves genre veterans and newcomers, chosen at the start. Difficulty comes from the club the player picks and a help setting that moves only explanation and pauses, never staff accuracy, results, or the AI. Every area can go to staff; for a newcomer, areas open over the first season, introduced by the staff who run them; and the assistant points each week to what matters. A sacked manager looks for a new job and the save goes on, and AI clubs copy what wins, so a winning idea stops being new.
- **Watching the match.** The base view is the top-down pitch with height, players as circles for now, with overlays, zoom, and follow. Match length comes from a five-mode ladder changeable mid-match, a dynamic mode, build-up before every moment, and custom rules, and the pitch fast-forwards between highlights. The player can rewind while paused and replay any part afterwards; other grounds show live scores and highlights after the whistle. Occasion comes from crowd sound, a pre-match build-up, and the pitch itself, and the realism test bands movement against tracking data.
- **Traits, personality, and chemistry.** A trait is a habit of play, and each trait happens about 1.5 to 2 times as often as in similar players. The thirteen personality attributes move cards, setbacks, leadership, and professionalism. Understanding between players and grasp of the tactic grow with time together, capped so a top player still stands out in five matches. A squad change costs little and recovers fast.
- **Psychological factors.** Good events lift and setbacks dip, and both fade within minutes. Pressure grows with the stakes, rivalry, and what a match means to a player. Players react to the score by personality within the manager's mentality. Comebacks come from real mechanisms, with no script, and each part has a band against real data.
- **Rules and officiating.** The engine plays the complete Laws of the Game with each league's differences. Fouls are graded, and the referee's strictness and the team's tackling move the cards. A video referee works by competition, and each competition has its own rule pack and tie-breakers. Each part is checked by scenarios or bands.
- **Goalkeepers.** The nine keeper attributes stay. A keeper's style comes from the keeper role, traits, and attributes, and distribution follows the team's build-up and a keeper instruction. Keepers are amplified so quality shows, while the league save rate stays at 65 to 75 percent.
- **Environment.** Rain and wind act through the ball physics under a cap, a poor or worn pitch widens touch and pass errors, and clubs can prepare the pitch. Home advantage scales with the crowd and stays inside its measured band.
- **Analytical models.** Thirteen models: six in players' choices plus defensive ghosting, three for AI and staff, and two for analysis. Each starts on real data and is refitted on the engine. Models that change play must beat simpler rules on realism and fit the budget. Display and staff models come early, and in-play models come last.
- **Technical robustness.** Matches replay identically everywhere, matchdays and seasons fit set limits on a reference laptop, replays keep their frames and saves migrate across updates, and developers can trace any result to its random draws. Old saves and replays are tested at every release.
- **Engine architecture.** Every part becomes a module behind a typed interface, chosen in configuration, reading a read-only view and proposing changes that only the loop applies. Ability factors are modifiers in the four families. One staged refactor to this contract comes before all the realism work, after the match-engine branch ships.

The work runs in order. Engine realism comes first, starting with the test harness and then team shape and the block. The event-contract redesign comes next, and the season layer comes last. All twelve pieces are now written as three design documents.

Session 8 explored the last open parts of the map. It opened match preparation, where knowing the opponent carries the area, the staff prepare by default, and the effect shows only where the squad fits the plan. Your top risk there became a new thread: staff needed properly decided attributes. Two researchers read what real clubs assess for about 25 roles, and you trimmed about 110 candidates into one shared list of 32 attributes that staff and managers both read, with the manager's old tactical attribute mapped onto it. The session then set difficulty as the club plus a help setting that only explains, limited the manager's time by the structure of the week, and designed match viewing around a top-down pitch with height, a five-mode highlight ladder, rewind, and movement bands. Every area is now explored, no tension or question is open, and all twelve pieces of work are stale. The sharpest risk is still load: the checks, the saves, and now 32 staff attributes, each with a season-long check. The coherence pass that followed read the design documents and the board against session 8. It merged three repeats, applied four later decisions, and settled ten conflicts and gaps with you: the briefing now moves compliance, burnout applies only to AI managers, tactical flexibility carries habit-breaking, ambition and loyalty split, staff carry a regional knowledge list, tactics have a limit and fade, and the emulator also screens the staff attributes. You then brought your twelfth brief, on traits, personality, and chemistry. All eleven criteria are covered, all three parts go into the first version, and the top risk is stacked effects swamping ability. Your thirteenth brief, on psychological factors, followed: all seven criteria are covered, all four parts go into the first version, and the top risks are double counting, thin data, and a stacked mind family. Your fourteenth brief, on rules, officiating, and formats, closed with all seven criteria covered and all of it in the first version. Your fifteenth brief, on goalkeepers, closed the same way, with keepers amplified so a top keeper stands out. Your sixteenth brief, on the environment, made weather and pitch the core. Your seventeenth brief became its own work group on analytical models, placed last. Your eighteenth brief, on technical robustness, closed with all ten criteria covered. Your nineteenth brief, on engine architecture, made a module refactor the new first piece of work.

Session 7 checked five of your briefs against the board: the data model, action resolution, calibration, tactical counterplay, and the engine structure. None was fully explored. For example, only 18 of the 36 attributes reached a match calculation, every effect was linear, and roles only helped the AI pick players. The session settled each brief and went beyond it where the brief touched other areas: team numbers, club identity, relationships, the laptop budget, skipped matches, and mid-match saves.

You then asked to bring the board into cohesion. The pass merged overlapping decisions, let the later decision win where two disagreed, and read the three design documents against session 7. The documents disagree in 18 places, so all twelve pieces of work are now stale. You settled three conflicts: error sources split the error band, tactic familiarity lives with the player, and the style expectation blends identity with the owner type. The fourth conflict was the check load: the checks chosen this session need about 1.5 million matches for a release run, while the player's matchday stays light. You settled it too. Each change runs about 1,000 matches, random streams split per player and action so paired seeds cut the noise, batches stop early when the result is clear, each milestone runs only the touched parts, and a release run finishes overnight on your Mac mini and VPS. Last, you added an engine emulator: a model fitted on spread-out full-engine runs that predicts the mean and spread of every banded statistic from the inputs. It tunes settings, screens sensitivity, and picks what each milestone runs, and the full engine still judges every band. Then it went deep on your sixth brief, player decisions and off-ball movement. The engine had none of it beyond a five-option carrier and fixed anchors, and the board settled perception, decision time, the option set, off-ball and defensive behaviour, transitions, keepers, instructions, traits, and the bands, and moved players far from the ball to a human think rate. Then it went deep on your tenth brief, fatigue, injuries, and congestion: every match started fresh and injuries were flat rolls, and the board designed reserves, recovery and load, injuries by exposure and type at real frequency, returns, history, and season bands, finding that real congestion costs injuries far more than running. Then it went deep on your eleventh brief, long-term integration: it connected each match to growth, form, reputation, value, the board, income, and job security, dampened the loops with real forces and a soft pull, and set a multi-season check, finding that real football shows no hot hand and that points predict next season worst. Then it went deep on your ninth brief, set pieces: the engine had no takers, routines, walls, marking, or aerial model, and the board designed all of them with bands that stop a runaway routine. Then it went deep on your seventh brief, in-game management. Changes landed in one step at a stoppage and nothing measured their effect; the board split changes by kind with uptake over time, gave the manager and the AI the same wide set of tools, information, and reaction time, dated the rules per competition, and checks every tool's effect on forked matches. Before that, it went deep on your eighth brief, output and presentation: a true-cause log, one record behind every figure with a test and a trace, fair ratings, ranked highlights, a researched catalogue of 180 statistics and visualisations, staff analysis at four times, richer commentary with sound, and a playtest scored against the true causes. The sharpest risk is now whether the realism work slows each match so much that overnight needs sampling.

Session 6 filled every empty area of the map. It settled the licences by accepting the risk, and it closed the small open questions of the design documents. It then explored the staff, contracts and the dressing room, supporters and the press, where new players come from, international football, and the manager's week, with seven researchers supplying real rules and figures. The twelve pieces of work from session 4 cover none of these new areas, so the next `done` must shape new work for them. One question is still open: how the manager's time is limited. The sharpest risk is size: nearly every answer this session added a full system, and each one adds work to the season layer and load to the player's machine. There is also a practical risk: the findings documents that every earlier piece of work reads exist only in a gitignored folder on one machine.

## Map

### The in-progress plans and what they miss — problem side — explored

The engine workflow keeps its charter and sixteen slices. Realism runs as a separate programme after it, with its own performance budget. Core: realism bands are outputs, never tuning targets. Order: test harness, then team shape, then the event contract, then the season layer. Scope: twelve pieces routed to three design documents.

### The player as a person: attributes, personality, body, positions — problem side — explored

A player becomes a person: a rating per position, a rating per foot, new performance attributes, twelve hidden personality traits, and learnable habits. Attribute effects are amplified beyond real sizes so quality is felt over about five matches. Personality is seen through scouting noise. Open: how the attribute and trait overlaps split in the engine.

### The club and the squad: identity, venue, mood, cohesion — problem side — explored

A club has a style from identity, squad, and tactic, a home ground, a reputation, a squad familiarity per tactic, and a team mood that personality shapes. Style clash follows the measured additive possession rule. Open: none central.

### Tactics beyond Tier 2: phases, transitions, set pieces, live changes — solution side — explored

Each team has an in-possession and an out-of-possession shape, set-piece routines, per-player and opposition instructions, and triggers. Live changes wait for the next stoppage, and both managers make them. Team shape and the block follow the measured slide with the ball. Open: the nine real phases against the two chosen.

### How the match plays: actions, choices, duels, the ball in the air, officials, injuries — solution side — explored

Every action family resolves from the situation first and the player second. The duel is one contested action among several. Play in the air uses the ball height the engine already has. A full referee with a video assistant, and injuries with a severity. Open: three action families have no outcome evidence.

### The realism test: bands, design targets, watched reviews — solution side — explored

The test bands measured per-match rates, scorelines, pass completion by kind, and joint spreads, with a real target and a design target where the game departs on purpose. People watch matches and seasons at milestones. StatsBomb and Transfermarkt stay, with the licence risk accepted. Open: none central.

### The manager as a modelled entity — problem side — explored

Both managers are entities with attributes. The human's tactical attribute moves information accuracy, not instructions. The AI manager picks tactics from philosophy, opponent, match state, and season situation. Open: none central.

### The season layer: competitions, careers, market, club business — solution side — explored

The season layer mirrors the European structure, with careers on per-attribute age curves, a market valued from visible data only, and a club business with a board and an owner. The fast model must pass the same season bands. Open: how development effects are sized, since no public data sizes them.

### The open questions the three design documents left (42 in all) — problem side — explored

The forty-two open questions of the three design documents were closed in session six, except the technical questions of the event contract, which go to that build step's plan.

### The staff around the squad: coaches, scouts, medical staff, as people with their own quality — problem side — explored

Every role a real club employs is a person with a rating that each club sees through whoever hires. Staff and managers share one list of 32 attributes, each role reading a weighted part, with suitability computed for every role; the players' twelve traits plus five staff traits; three hidden values led by consistency; a languages list; and ten leaning tags. Staff grow by work, courses, and licences. Core: all of it. Scope: keep all, in the first version.

### Contracts and the dressing room: negotiation, promises, agents, squad status, unrest — problem side — explored

Contracts are negotiated by agent, director of football, manager, and family, and carry money, bonuses, clauses, and promises. The dressing room has standing, groups, a culture, and a view of the manager. Unrest escalates and has a small on-pitch effect. Discipline follows each country's rules.

### Supporters and the press: fan mood, expectations, interviews, media pressure — problem side — explored

Supporters are groups whose balance is the fan mood, with power through the board, ownership models, money, and the manager. The crowd moves the referee and nervous players. Press conferences, journalists as people, and stories that build over weeks. Open: press duties by competition.

### International football: national teams, international breaks, call-ups, tournaments — problem side — explored

The calendar, national teams, and players on duty are in the game; managing a nation comes later. Call-ups follow form, the coach's taste, loyalty, and depth. Duty brings fatigue, injuries with compensation, mood, and value changes.

### How the player watches the match: real time, key moments, other grounds — solution side — explored

The base view is the top-down pitch with height, players as circles for now. Overlays toggle, and the player can zoom and follow. Length comes from a five-mode ladder, a dynamic mode, build-up before each moment, and custom rules; between highlights the pitch fast-forwards. Rewind while paused and full replays after. Other grounds: live scores, highlights after. Occasion: sound, then build-up, then the pitch. Movement is banded against tracking data. Core: all of it. Scope: keep all, in the first version.

### New players entering the world: youth academies, youth intake, and generated players — problem side — explored

Academies follow each country's structure, the full model starts at about 16, and a young player's future is a narrowing range. The generator copies measured patterns: birth quarters, late bloomers, national output. Style comes from where a player develops. Open: versatility and parent rates.

### The manager's week: information, time, team talks, and delegation — problem side — explored

Team talks move mood and focus; delegation works the same in every area; information comes through a filtered inbox, reports, and escalations, and some messages need a reply by a deadline. Time is limited by the week's structure: training slots, one briefing per match, team talks, press conferences, a few one-to-one talks, a staff meeting, and trips; no hour budget and no burnout. A task done personally uses the manager's attributes and builds bonds. Core: all of it. Scope: keep all, in the first version.

### The data model as an engine contract: attribute jobs, scale, visible and hidden, effective ability, role weights, sensitivity — problem and solution side — explored

Every attribute keeps a job; actions run in stages with blended, fitted weights. Tenths behind 1 to 20 for every rating in the game, a convex curve set by the five-match target. A bounded effective-ability layer, hidden values in words. About 40 roles with three behaviours. Team numbers act on team actions; identity acts off the pitch. Height, age, and adaptation act; build is a word. Relationships carry two numbers. A sensitivity test covers attributes, settings, roles, team numbers, and, over seasons, staff ratings. Core: all of it. Scope: keep all; eight design-document pieces are stale, to rewrite at the next distil.

### Action resolution and the probability layer: probabilities, shots, passes, duels, errors, variance — problem and solution side — explored

Each contact (tackle, first touch, keeper handling, strike, pass, interception) draws from the situation and the attributes; physics carries the ball between contacts. A strike takes two draws; a keeper's reach is physics and the handling a draw; penalties add a guessing game. Duels run in two steps with four outcomes. Errors come from draw tails, lapses, and named events. Checks: xG buckets by a real-data model, shot split, pass curves, duel set, Opta errors, and variance with real and design targets. Core: all of it. Scope: keep all.

### Machine load and save size: the cost of the realism additions on the player's machine — problem side — explored

The game promises to run well on an ordinary laptop, with fixed limits the player feels and a relative limit per match in the tests. No realism lever is pulled; when the budget fails, engineering optimises first and comes back only if it still fails. Open: the limit numbers, for the plan.

### Realism calibration and validation: per-match targets, distributions, league tables, divisions, the harness — solution side — explored

Targets from five recent seasons in three layers: per league with data, per tier, and banding groups for the rest; a division's style emerges from its players. Tolerance is the real spread plus sampling noise. Every scoreline and whole distributions are checked, and the league table in full. Each change runs a few thousand matches; each release a full run; every report is stored and compared. Leagues without data sit in banding groups fed by the tier formula. Core: all of it. Scope: keep all.

### Tactical counterplay and team-level AI: costs, counters, squad fit, shape, transitions, AI managers — problem and solution side — explored

Costs, counters, and squad fit emerge from play through the players, with no table and no fit number. Rest defence follows duties, and line gaps emerge. A round-robin of eight named styles runs on every change and a setting grid before release; every preset needs a clear weakness. AI managers use staff advice and memory, carry a scoutable fingerprint, and are checked for fit and the possession rule. Paired squads check fit. Beyond the brief: the AI learns in the match and an exploit hunt runs before release; players manage games with instructions on top; touchline shouts; conditions change which tactic works. Core: all of it. Scope: keep all.

### Match engine structure: two models, phases, pitch, clock, seeded replay — solution side — explored

The full engine runs every active-league match; a fast model runs the rest, fitted from the full engine after each change and checked by season bands and per pairing. A written phase machine with a position check at each restart. Grounds differ in size within the laws. Added time prices each event kind, video reviews included. Replay is identical on every supported machine. Beyond the brief: a skip finishes on the fast model from the current state; background matches run first and reveal their events in step; every player thinks every tick; a mid-match save finishes on its old engine. Core: all of it. Scope: keep all.

### Cohesion across areas: the decisions against each other and against the design documents — problem and solution side — explored

Raised in session seven when you asked to bring the board into cohesion. The pass read the three design documents against session seven, compared the new areas with the earlier ones, and priced the checks. It merged overlapping decisions, applied the later decision where two disagreed, and brought four real conflicts to you. Three are settled: errors split their budget between sources, tactic familiarity lives with the player and the team value is derived, and the style expectation blends identity with the owner type. The fourth was the check load: about 1.5 million matches for a release run. It is settled: about 1,000 matches on each change, split random streams so paired seeds work, early stopping, only the touched parts of the heavy checks at each milestone, and a full release run overnight on the Mac mini and the VPS. An engine emulator, fitted on spread-out full-engine runs, now tunes settings, screens sensitivity, and triages each milestone, and the full engine still judges every band.

### Player decision-making and off-ball movement: options, perception, time under pressure, runs, defending, traits — problem and solution side — explored

Raised in session seven from your sixth brief. The engine today scores five options for the carrier, who sees everything, and moves everyone else to a formation anchor with no runs and no defensive jobs. The board now sets: a picture per player refreshed by timed scans, holding motion and team-mates' calls; decision time before a choice and commit time after it; the whole 53-action catalogue plus game extras, scored as expected value with weights fitted to real choices, and a poor decision-maker going wrong through noisy estimates and a soft pick; off-ball movement as anchor, role patterns, and utility, with runners sharing space by patterns, claims, calls, and space value; defending as a utility inside the team's marking system, with claims and calls; transitions as utility inside a counter-press window; keepers on the same machinery with the measured depth rule; instructions as limits and weights, followed as faithfully as a player's decisions and teamwork allow; traits that bias and style actions and show in reports; players near the ball thinking every tick and others at a human rate; and shape, crowding, behaviour, frozen-player, and crowded-pass bands. Core: all of it. Scope: keep all.

### Output and presentation: commentary, highlights, statistics, ratings, staff explanations, consistency — problem and solution side — explored

Raised in session seven from your eighth brief. Today the game shows team totals, 168 template lines of text commentary with no shots or passes, no ratings, no highlight ranking, no staff analysis, and counts some figures on two paths. The board now sets: a true-cause log that staff read through their quality and that scores the playtest; figures and visualisations always true, staff readings fallible by quality, advice fallible, and staff setting depth while the manager's attribute acts on readings only; text commentary with sound from AI-written, reviewed, tagged banks with some grammar, drawing on player history, the season, match threads, and causes, in English first; ratings from action value plus off-ball credit, fair to each role; highlights by win-probability swing, manager moments, and story; a wide, live set of visualisations from a 180-item catalogue, on designed, customisable screens that analysts also fill; a provider-style xG and one definition per metric; one record for everything, a recompute test, and a trace from every claim to its moments; staff analysis live, at half-time, after the match, and across matches; four reading aids; fast-model matches with banded per-player statistics; decision internals kept for all recent full-engine matches; and a playtest on planted and ordinary matches with what-if predictions. Core: all of it. Scope: keep all.

### In-game management: touchline changes, substitutions, live information, speed and pause, proportionate effects, AI parity — problem and solution side — explored

Raised in session seven from your seventh brief. Today a live change is one patch applied in full at the next stoppage, one rule pack serves every competition with no concussion substitutes, the manager sees nine team figures and energy bars, the viewer runs at up to 8 times with its own pause, the AI acts on fixed minutes without changing formation, and nothing measures a change's effect. The board now sets: substitutions and formation changes at a stoppage, and shouts and instructions at once with uptake over time; free pause and chosen automatic pauses; an AI with the same rules, information, and reaction time, using every tool as well as its skill allows; effects that emerge, banded against real game-state data, hunted for exploits, and each with a cost; dated rule packs per competition; head knocks assessed by medics and decided by the manager; live player state, an opponent read, change feedback, and advice with options; team, opposition, and player instructions and game-state plans; a forked-match check over every tool; and plans and triggers applying after a skip. Core: all of it. Scope: keep all.

### Set pieces: routines, takers, defensive setups, aerial contests, penalties, throw-ins — problem and solution side — explored

Raised in session seven from your ninth brief. Today the nearest player takes almost every restart, a corner is one fixed cross, there are no walls, marking, or aerial model, penalties convert below real rates, and a stored baseline shows almost no corners because nothing blocks shots yet. The board now sets: routines from a library, an editor, and the set-piece coach; named takers per kind and side who choose among options in play; defensive jobs within a marking system, with defenders reacting to what they see; first contact from the delivery's physics, runs, height, jumping, and a heading duel, plus blocks and holding the referee may penalise; long-throw, retention, and quick throw-ins; routine familiarity with a limit on how many a squad holds; quick restarts that defenders can obstruct; per-routine bands, opponents who learn, and execution error against runaway routines; a set-piece share band per league with drift; penalty bands per league with a shoot-out pressure curve; and a coach effect up to the real range, with the team spread banded. Core: all of it. Scope: keep all.

### Fatigue, injuries, and fixture congestion: in-match decline, recovery, sharpness, injury risk and types, rotation — problem and solution side — explored

Raised in session seven from your tenth brief, then taken deeper and zoomed out. Today a player has one energy value per match, injuries are flat rolls with no severity, and every match starts every player fresh. The board now sets, in the match: a sprint reserve, a slow reserve, and mental fatigue, kept modest; pacing by reserves; role-specific drain, fresh substitutes, extra time about 10 percent below regulation, and small recoveries in breaks. Between matches: condition over about three days and an accumulated load seen through staff; training at a daily rhythm, a preseason base the off-season loses, and staff who design or warn; sharpness from matches by level and from match-simulation training; travel load, jet lag by time zone, climate, and travel planning; illness at real rates that can spread. Injuries: a hazard per exposure event with real types, layoffs, recurrence, and history by site; training injuries; real frequency; treatment choices where the manager, the player, and the agent have a say; returns through phases; knocks that carry and hide; and a mind that dips, stays cautious, and suffers when injured long. Beyond: congestion costing mostly injury risk; rotation pushed by morale, sharpness, the calendar, and staff; medicals and lower values for injury history; crisis consequences for staff, players, press, and fans; the same state in fast-model leagues; bands judged on full-engine seasons with the emulator between releases; and a save that keeps a window by activity. Core: all of it. Scope: keep all.

### Long-term integration: how each match feeds the season and the career, and how loops are dampened — problem and solution side — explored

Raised in session seven from your eleventh brief. The board now sets how each match feeds the wider game: growth in three layers (training base, minutes at the right challenge, a performance extra); form at two speeds with a fading memory; a board that reads points against expected, trend, big matches, and style; reputation from level-weighted ratings, big stages, a visible-stats bias, and a slow fade; value with a slow core and a fast edge; income from merit money, lagged attendance, sponsors by reputation, and European money; and job security from earned credit, rising targets, owner changes, and real AI sackings. Loops are dampened: modest banded mood with complacency and opponents who lift; real league rules, wage pressure, ageing and poaching, and rivals who react; balance that emerges plus a soft pull in AI choices; and four recovery routes. Every match keeps stakes, secondary goals, dead-rubber effects, and stories. A multi-season check of ten fifty-season saves bands title turnover, fortune churn, and up and down rates against a pooled band, and four guards stop long-save drift. Core: all of it. Scope: keep all.

### Preparing for the next match: opposition scouting, the match plan, and the training focus of the week — problem and solution side — explored

Knowing the opponent carries the area; the staff prepare the plan, the training week, and the briefing by default, and the manager edits. Rehearsal gives a grasp that fades after the match, and the briefing moves faithfulness, each player's picture, and mood. Preparation helps only where the squad fits, costs staff workload and training time, and has a wide band set after playtests. Surprises come from real change, adaptability, disguise, and leaks; AI managers read the manager's whole fingerprint. Core: all of it. Scope: keep all, in the first version.

### Challenge and the player's experience: difficulty, learning the game, and how much realism the player sees — problem side — explored

The game answers overwhelm, losing without knowing why, easy once solved, and early sacking. A new save asks whether the player knows the genre. Difficulty comes from the club and a help setting that moves only explanation and pauses. Every area can go to staff; newcomers see areas open over the first season; the assistant points each week. A sacked manager finds a new job, and AI clubs copy what wins. Core: all of it. Scope: keep all, in the first version.

### Player traits, personality, and chemistry: decision weights, personality effects, familiarity, and how strong they are — problem and solution side — explored

Traits are habits of play from the genre list of about fifty; a player carries any number but never two opposite traits, and each trait happens about 1.5 to 2 times as often as in similar players without it. Personality attributes move cards within the real spread, setback dips, leader calls, and professionalism, each with its own check. Understanding between players and grasp of the tactic grow through training and matches, capped by the five-match test; a squad change costs little and recovers fast, checked by a rotation band. Core: all three equally. Scope: keep all, in the first version. Top risk: stacked effects.

### Psychological factors: momentum, pressure from match importance, game-state behaviour, and comebacks — problem and solution side — explored

Momentum: good events lift confidence and setbacks dip it, a home crowd adds to the lift, and both fade within minutes, checked by a next-goal band. Pressure: match importance comes from the stakes, rivalry, and what the match means to the player; it harms nervous players and lifts big-match players a little, checked by a big-match band. Game state: players read the score by personality, leaning within the manager's mentality, checked by possession and shot shares cut by margin, period, and importance. Comebacks come from eight mechanisms with no script, checked by a comeback band. Core: all equally. Scope: keep all, in the first version. Top risks: double counting, thin data, and a stacked mind family.

### Rules, officiating, and competition formats: the Laws, referee profiles, the video referee, and competition rules — problem and solution side — explored

The engine implements the complete IFAB Laws with each league's differences, including every offside edge case, checked by a scenario per clause, bands on offsides, penalties, and free kicks, and a review of sampled calls. Each foul is graded careless, reckless, or excessive force, context cards apply, and the referee's strictness moves the lines; tackling is set by team and player instructions; a referee spread band checks cards and fouls. The video referee is checked on overturns, delay, and missed errors. Each competition has a full rule pack and its real tie-breakers. Core: all equally. Scope: keep all, in the first version. Top risk: none named.

### Goalkeeper specifics: keeper attributes, rebounds, distribution, save rate, and keeper styles — problem and solution side — explored

The nine keeper attributes stay, and the profile shows positioning, distribution, and sweeping. A keeper's style comes from the keeper role, traits, and attributes. Distribution follows the team's build-up instruction and a keeper instruction for targets. Keeper effects are amplified like other players', so a top keeper stands out, while the league save rate stays at 65 to 75 percent. Four bands check keepers: save rate, goals prevented, rebound shots by handling, and sweeper actions. Core: all equally. Scope: keep all, in the first version. Top risk: none named.

### Environmental factors: weather, pitch quality, and home advantage with crowd size — problem and solution side — explored

Rain and wind act through the ball physics, and a cap keeps each effect small. A poor pitch widens touch and pass errors, pitches wear through the season, and a club can prepare its pitch for its tactic. Home advantage scales with attendance and fullness and falls with away fans; travel distance has only a very small effect. Each condition has its own band, with a limit on its distance from the dry band. Core: weather and pitch. Scope: keep all, in the first version. Top risks: caps with no real source, and home advantage counted twice.

### Advanced analytical models: pitch control, expected threat, and possession value in play and in analysis — problem and solution side — explored

A separate work group of thirteen models. In play: expected threat and possession value, pitch control in each player's scanned picture, pass completion, off-ball scoring opportunity, receiver prediction, and a pressing model, plus defensive ghosting. For AI and staff: win probability, an Elo-style team rating, and style and role detection. For analysis: action value and decision quality. Each model starts on real data and is refitted on the engine's matches after each change. Models that change play must pass the comparison test (closer to real on progression and choice match, inside the budget); display models pass the one-record check. The display and staff models come early; the in-play models and ghosting come last. Full models run in the watched match and a stand-in elsewhere. Top risks: threat may make choices less real, cost, and thin freeze-frame and tracking data.

### Technical robustness: determinism, performance, exploit testing, data sourcing, versioning, and debugging — solution side — explored

Matches replay identically on every machine from fixed steps, a stored seed, and split random streams. One matchday of every active league must finish within the matchday wait and a full season within its limit on the reference laptop, and a release scan fails on licensed data. Replays store their frames, seed, and version; saves migrate forward; every release loads old saves and plays old replays. Developers get a draw trace, a bisect tool, and in-game bug reports. Retired people archive, and a 50-season check guards speed and save size. The exploit hunt runs before release. Core: all equally. Scope: keep all, in the first version. Top risk: none named.

### Match engine architecture: modules, interfaces, state, modifiers, configuration, and testing — solution side — explored

Every part of the engine becomes a module behind a typed interface, registered by name and chosen per slot in configuration. Modules read a read-only view and return proposed changes that only the loop applies; each optional module has an off version; ability factors are modifiers grouped in the four families. The quick engine stays a fitted model but emits the same event types. Each module carries a card with its calibration source. Types enforce the read-only view, an off-switch run and a swap test check the contract, and one staged refactor to the contract comes before the realism work, after the match-engine branch ships: streams split and a new baseline first, then parts one at a time, pure parts first and referee rules and possession last. Scope: keep, first in the order of work. Top risk: effects that cross module lines.

### What goes wrong with the realism test and tuning today — problem side — touched
Raised in session 10. Today sixteen bands pass on fixed ranges, the sampling error decides nothing, hand tuning fixes one band and breaks others, and a full run takes about 80 minutes. You said all four failures matter, and invisible noise can wait longest. Open: none central.

### The statistical model of real football that the test compares against — solution side — explored
A fitted model judges results and every team count per strength gap, with strength that changes in a season, a draw correction, and league adjustments. Real and generated teams are rated by the same fit; results per gap match real football, and the strength spread may be wider as a design target. Timing is judged by ranges. Open: the player-data sources. Scope: kept, all in the first version.

### The test harness: pass rules, change checks, many bands, and its place in the new architecture — solution side — explored
A band passes by an equivalence test with a margin per band sized so a sound engine passes 97 percent of runs; rare bands get wider margins in change runs, and design bands a cap on distance from real. A change run plays at least 12,000 matches within ten minutes against a stated intent. One registry holds every band; slow tests and module cards read it; a shift detector watches drift. Open: none central.

### Realism beyond averages: whole distributions, movement, and play that looks right — solution side — explored
Whole distributions are compared at fixed percentiles as bands, and a movement score rates engine movement against tracking data, free now and licensed before release. Every tactic setting is tested by a covering array over both teams, with four checks per tactic and an exploit search, and Nash averaging rates tactics against the field. Blind clips and a real-squad back-test are out. Scope: kept, all core and in the first version.

### Fast iteration: feedback tiers, tuning many settings at once, and the emulator — solution side — explored
Values with their own data are fitted bottom-up; only values with no data are searched, on the full engine, against a fresh hold-out per run with a summed loss. The emulator screens sensitivity and picks milestone runs. Feedback comes in four steps: seconds, a ten-minute change run, milestones, and a release run of days. Scope: kept, all in the first version.

### Performance for fast iteration: today's limits, the expectations, and how to get the speed — problem and solution side — explored
A replay check guards every speed change; result-identical speed-ups (about twice the speed, measured) and cross-machine fixes go first; a lean harness with an old-engine cache; three noise methods, grouped bands, a simultaneous test, and a strength curve; tactics cells stop early and pair only interacting roles, with a predictor for the release array; realism under one shared step budget. Scope: kept, all in the first version.

## Open now

- Top risk of the staff area: check load, save size, role weights to tune, and effects with no measured size, all at once.
- Top risk of traits, personality, and chemistry: stacked effects swamping ability.
- Top risks of psychological factors: double counting when behind, thin data for the finely cut bands, and a stacked mind family.
- Top risks of environmental factors: weather and pitch caps with no real source, and home advantage counted twice.
- Top risks of the analytical models: threat may make choices less real, cost, and thin freeze-frame data.
- Top risk of the engine architecture: effects that cross module lines.
- Session 10 opened five areas on the realism test: what goes wrong today, the statistical model of real football, pass rules and change checks, realism beyond averages, and fast iteration.
- Open tension: the rule that bands are never tuning targets pulls against the emulator's role as a tuning tool.
- Open tension: the design documents state fixed real ranges, while the board set tolerance as real spread plus sampling noise.
- Open tension: a release run of about 1.5 million matches takes about 33 hours at today's engine speed on 8 cores.
- Top risk of session 10: both verdict budgets need about twelve times today's harness throughput.
- Open: judging one change, realism beyond averages, and fast tuning were not yet discussed.
- All twelve pieces of work are stale and must be reshaped at the next done.

## Briefs

- Player traits, personality, and chemistry: 11 covered, 0 partial, 0 open, 0 out of scope.
- Psychological factors: 7 covered, 0 partial, 0 open, 0 out of scope.
- Rules, officiating, and competition formats: 7 covered, 0 partial, 0 open, 0 out of scope.
- Goalkeeper specifics: 7 covered, 0 partial, 0 open, 0 out of scope.
- Environmental factors: 6 covered, 0 partial, 0 open, 0 out of scope.
- Advanced analytical models: 7 covered, 0 partial, 0 open, 0 out of scope.
- Technical robustness: 10 covered, 0 partial, 0 open, 0 out of scope.
- Match engine architecture: 16 covered, 0 partial, 0 open, 0 out of scope.
- Rapid realism iteration and an engine emulator (older report): 24 covered, 0 partial, 0 open, 2 out of scope (forked saved-state tests, because full matches only stays; a fixed first emulator campaign, because its domain is chosen later).

## Sessions

### Session 10 — 2026-09-25
We set out to look at the realism test itself: how the harness judges a band, what it compares the engine against, and how fast a developer learns whether a change helped. We learned that today the bands pass on fixed ranges while the sampling error decides nothing, that hand tuning fixes one band and breaks another, and that a full run takes about 80 minutes. You said all four failures matter. You chose a split yardstick: a model fitted on five real seasons judges results and the table, measured ranges judge the rest, and both compare per gap in team strength, because league totals are fair only when the generated leagues copy the real spread. A band passes only when its whole interval sits inside a margin set as a share of the real spread, every band can block, and the developer decides a not-sure result. You kept both the five-minute verdict and the every-band check, so the harness needs about twelve times today's speed. Still open: how one change is judged, checks beyond averages, and tuning many settings without gaming the bands. The next sitting starts with judging one change.

The stories of sessions 1 to 9 are told in the summary above.

## Decisions

### The realism test, the statistical model, and fast iteration (session 10)

- The realism test must answer all four failures: bands that fight each other under hand tuning, a slow verdict, noise that the pass rule cannot see, and play that looks wrong while the averages pass. Why: The person named all four as true today.
- A developer learns within about five minutes whether a change made realism better or worse on the bands it touches. Why: A coffee-length wait keeps the loop fast enough to tune by, and about 3,800 matches fit at today speed. Accepted risk: The budget holds about 3,800 matches at today speed; if the realism work halves the engine speed, it holds about 1,900, and the per-change check must shrink or stop earlier.
- The talk goes into the statistical model and the pass rule first, as the foundation for the change check, tuning, and the checks beyond averages.
- Of the four failures, invisible noise can wait longest: the first version of the new harness may keep today fixed ranges while the other failures are answered. Accepted risk: Every other tool reads the pass rule, so until the noise fix lands the change check and the tuning can chase noise, and a band can drift inside its fixed range unseen.
- The yardstick is split: a statistical model fitted on the five real seasons judges goals, scorelines, results, and the table, and measured ranges judge passes, fouls, cards, running, and the other statistics. Why: The published models are strong for results, and weaker for the other counts. Accepted risk: Two kinds of band to build and explain, and an error in the fitted model becomes an error in the test.
- The yardstick compares results and goals for each gap in team strength, so real and generated teams both carry a strength rating; this fits the three-layer league bands, the fast-model check per pairing, and the fitted results model. Why: League totals are fair only when the generated leagues copy the real strength spread. Accepted risk: A strength rating is needed for every real team in the five seasons and for every generated team. This replaced: compare whole-league totals only.
- A band passes by an equivalence test: the whole confidence interval of the engine statistic must sit inside the band margin, so a small or noisy run cannot pass by luck and may return not sure yet.
- Every band can block a change; there are no watch-only bands. Accepted risk: Under a strict family rule each band needs about 2.4 times the matches of a single test at 300 bands, so the five-minute budget cannot hold every band in one change run.
- Every change run judges every band, and the wait for a verdict grows to about an hour. Accepted risk: Nothing slips past a change, but the loop is slow, and an hour holds about 45,000 matches at today speed.
- Each band margin is a fixed share of how much real leagues and seasons differ on that statistic, for example half, set in advance and never after a run.
- When a band comes back not sure yet, the developer sees the interval and the margin and decides whether the change goes ahead. Accepted risk: The call depends on judgement under time pressure, and a hurried yes can let a bad change sit until the overnight run.
- Both budgets hold: a verdict on every band within about five minutes, which makes the harness fast enough through more cores, a lighter measuring build, and cached baselines, a performance project of its own. Accepted risk: This needs about 12 times today throughput before the realism work slows each match, and until that project lands neither budget is met.

### The in-progress plans and what they miss

- Every realism band is an output that the mechanisms must produce, never a value the engine is tuned to hit directly. The watched-match reviews catch what the bands miss (a review after each new mechanism, where the reviewer compares a simulated match with a real match replayed in the same 2D view, works through a checklist of named qualities, and judges openly what looks wrong), and a failed review reopens the question of whether more detail in the planned model produces football that reads as real. Why: Asked how to settle whether more detail produces real-looking football, the person chose a principle plus the watched review rather than waiting for a proof.
- The person's thought is three readings at once: the planned model needs more detail inside it, the numbers can pass while the football is still missing, and the realism test is too weak to catch either. Why: Asked which reading of the realism gap was theirs, the person answered with a mix of readings rather than a single one.
- Every idea on this board belongs to a second workflow, a realism programme started separately from the match-engine workflow already in flight. Why: Form and season context need a season model that the engine workflow excludes, so the ideas are kept out of the build in flight.
- The distillation produces several candidates split by dependency, one per coherent piece, rather than one large candidate. Why: So the cheapest pieces can start first.
- The realism work splits into two programmes: engine realism (the player as a person, the club and squad, Tier 3 tactics, conditions and officials, the rebuilt realism test, and both managers as entities) and the season layer that earns form. The engine-facing pieces start while the season layer is still being shaped. Why: The season layer had grown into a full management game with a calendar, cups, a table, and season-long availability, larger than the engine it was meant to follow, so one second workflow no longer fitted.
- Touchline is one product, a football-management game, and the match engine is its first component; the workflow in flight is stage one of a longer programme rather than the whole thing.
- The match-engine workflow in flight keeps its charter and its exclusions unchanged. The exclusions describe that workflow correctly even though they no longer describe the product, and splitting the work into an engine-realism programme and a season-layer programme is what keeps both statements true. Why: The exclusions still describe that workflow correctly, and the two-programme split keeps both descriptions true, so the work in flight pays nothing.
- The engine-realism programme builds team shape and the block first, following the measured law that the team slides with the ball and changes width by phase, so the first watched-match review judges the cheapest mechanism that changes how every match looks. Why: It is the cheapest mechanism that changes how every match looks, so the first review judges something visible early.
- The season-layer piece of work no longer stays one card: it splits into smaller cards that can each start on their own, and the split is written at the next distillation.
- Engine realism runs first and the season layer follows, as the decisions to put the realism work in a second workflow and to split it into two programmes already set it, so the four season cards (competitions, squad and careers, the market, and club business) build on a realistic match. Why: So the season cards build on a match that is already realistic.
- The card that asked when the player record changes shape becomes a recorded decision rather than a card, because the board already answered it: the record changes when the realism programme starts. The card to rebuild the realism test is rewritten at the next distillation to the test the board now holds: bands for per-match rates, scorelines and home advantage, pass completion, and physical output; a separate test-only output with per-player data; one internal match record behind both outputs; a watched-match review after each new mechanism with three comparisons; and bands plus a person's reading for player development and for club finances. Why: The player-record question was already answered, so it needs no investigation run, and the realism-test card no longer matched what the board had decided.

### The player as a person: attributes, personality, body, positions

- A player carries at least eight personality traits (professionalism, temperament, ambition and loyalty, leadership, determination, adaptability, sportsmanship, and pressure handling), each a hidden value on the 1-to-20 scale stored in tenths, and still more traits are wanted. Core.
- The new performance attributes are marking, off-ball movement, set-piece skills (corners, free kicks, penalties, and long throws), and flair, which with 36 attributes today stays under the 50-attribute limit. Consistency joins the hidden values. Big-match nerve is left to the pressure-handling trait. Core.
- Where an attribute and a personality trait overlap, they split by scope: the attribute acts on each action in a match (composure on a shot under pressure, aggression on a tackle), and the trait acts on occasions and choices (a final, a penalty shoot-out, whether to dive or to foul cynically). Why: Otherwise the engine would read two values for one behaviour without saying what each one moves that the other does not.
- Four more traits join the eight already chosen, for twelve in all: versatility (willingness to play out of position), controversy (trouble off the pitch), money motivation (how much wages decide moves), and bravery (readiness for a 50-50, a header in traffic, or a block).
- The player record gains a fifth attribute group, personality, with its own slots outside the 50-attribute limit, so the twelve traits stop competing with new performance attributes for the 13 free slots. The record changes when the realism programme starts, as the decision on when the player record changes shape allows. Why: The twelve traits alone would take 12 of the 13 free slots and leave one slot for every new performance attribute.
- All four absent player fields matter: personality and traits; condition, morale, and form; age, height, and preferred foot; and positional familiarity.
- A player carries a competence rating for every position the player can fill, not one position code, so picking a lineup is a trade-off rather than slot-filling. Why: Picking a lineup should be a trade-off between players rather than filling slots.
- The gap between a strong player and a weak one is three things together: fewer errors, a higher ceiling of attempted actions, and more consistency. Why: Any one of the three on its own would read as a flat multiplier.
- A player carries a mood that the match itself moves, and that mood drives the player's decisions.
- Personality decides how strongly the player's own mood and the team's mood change that player's decisions; personality is the mediator, not a third mood.
- Clubs disagree about a player's worth for two reasons at once: each club sees a noisy estimate of the attributes, set by its scouting quality, and each club weighs the attributes differently by its playing identity and its manager's attributes. Why: If every club saw every attribute exactly, no club could value a player differently from another, and the market would have no disagreement to trade on.
- The player record changes shape when the realism programme starts and not before, following the precedent set for the event contract, which gains its player identifier only when the season-layer work starts.
