- In this work group, full models run in the watched match and a cheap stand-in everywhere else, an exception to the full-engine rule. Accepted risk: a result may depend on whether the match was watched.
- The analytical-models work group comes last in the order of work.
- Four more in-play models: pass completion, off-ball scoring opportunity, receiver prediction, and a turnover and pressing model.
- Four models for AI managers and staff: defensive ghosting, in-match win probability, an Elo-style team rating, and style and role detection.
- Two analysis models: action value as the base of ratings, and decision quality, which shows the option a player missed.
- Each model starts on real data and is refitted on the engine's matches after each change, with the comparison test watching for drift.
- Only models that change play pass the comparison test and the budget; display models pass the one-record check.
- The display and staff models come early; the in-play models and ghosting stay in the last work group.
- PFF's World Cup tracking is used under the same accepted licence risk as StatsBomb: derived models only, no data shipped. Accepted risk: its terms are unpublished.
- Defensive ghosting aims for the deep imitation model on the 64 PFF matches. Accepted risk: tournament football may not describe club defending.

### Technical robustness

- A replay stores its frames and its seed and version; saves carry a version and migrate forward. Accepted risk: larger replays, and old engines kept for debugging.
- Developers get a draw trace, a bisect tool across engine versions, and an in-game bug report with save, seed, and version.
- Retired and inactive people move to compact archive records, and old match detail compacts to summaries.
- One matchday of every active league must finish within the matchday wait and a full season within its limit on the reference laptop; a release scan fails on licensed data.
- Every release loads old saves and plays a season, plays old replays and re-simulates them on the old engine, and the 50-season check guards speed and save size.
- All parts of technical robustness are the core equally, all in the first version. No top risk named.

### Match engine architecture

- Every part is a module behind a typed interface; modules read a read-only view and propose changes, and only the loop applies them; optional modules have an off version. Accepted risk: an extra apply step on every tick.
- Each ability factor is a modifier that names its family; families combine their modifiers, then combine softly.
- The quick engine stays a fitted model but emits the same event types; it shares no calculation modules, a deliberate departure from the brief.
- A configuration file names the module and version per slot, and each module carries a card that a check requires to be complete.
- The contract is checked by types (read-only view), an off-switch run for every optional module and modifier, and a swap test per slot.
- One refactor moves the engine to the module contract before the realism work starts. Accepted risk: the realism work waits for it.
- The refactor runs in stages: split the random streams, recalibrate, and freeze a baseline; then move parts one at a time, each holding the baseline exactly; pure parts first, referee rules and possession last; hidden settings go.
- The match-engine workflow finishes its review and hands off as it is; the refactor starts afterwards as its own workflow.


### The statistical model of real football that the test compares against (session 11)
- The fitted model gives each team an attack and a defence number plus home advantage, with the Dixon-Coles low-score correction; a full hierarchical model is not used. Why: The comparison runs per strength gap, where the plain model under-predicts draws, and a full hierarchical model adds work with little gain for a test.
- Real and generated teams get their strength the same way: the same results model is fitted to a real season and to an engine season, and results are compared per gap in fitted strength; the engine's fitted strengths must also spread like the real ones. Why: No formula from player ratings to strength is needed, and the spread check comes almost free. Accepted risk: Strength is read from the results under test, so an engine that makes results too random also shrinks its own strength gaps; only the spread check catches that.
- The fitted model stays at whole-match level; goals by quarter of an hour, the effect of the score, and the effect of a red card are judged by measured ranges. Why: Timing inside the model makes it much harder to fit.
- The results model is fitted on five seasons from football-data.co.uk. Why: It has the widest coverage and clean files with cards and odds. Accepted risk: Its commercial terms are unclear, the same risk accepted for StatsBomb and Transfermarkt; the game ships none of the data, but a complaint means refitting on another source.
- The engine's results per strength gap must match real football, with no design target; the amplification of player quality must show in player numbers, not in how often favourites win. Why: Results are the core of realism, and real football is about 60 percent luck over a season.
- One results model is fitted across all leagues, with a small adjustment per league for home advantage and scoring. Why: It is steadier for small leagues and fits the three-layer targets.
- Results per strength gap stay real, but the spread of fitted team strengths may be wider than in real football, with a real target and a design target on the strength-spread check, so amplified player quality can make the best and worst teams sit further apart. Why: It keeps a top player felt without making favourites win more often than real teams of that strength. Accepted risk: Leagues become slightly more predictable than real ones: the title and relegation are decided by wider gaps.
- The yardstick model lets each team's strength change through a season, and the check tracks the engine's teams the same way. Why: Real teams rise and fall through a season.
- The parts that change slowly, such as the draw correction and the spread of goals, are fitted on a long history weighted towards recent seasons; the five recent seasons fit the parts that moved, such as home advantage. Why: Five seasons fix league-wide figures well, but a longer history steadies the slow parts.
- Strength comes from goals, not from shots on target, and home advantage is per league, not per club. Why: Shots-based strength would inherit any error in the engine's shot counts, and a club's home advantage is too noisy from about 19 home matches.
- The fitted model covers results and every team count in the football-data.co.uk files: shots, shots on target, corners, fouls, and cards, each with its own team strengths and real spread, compared per strength gap. Why: A range for the whole league cannot say whether a given team's counts are right. Accepted risk: Five more fitted parts to build, check, and explain, and the joint links between them still need the copula work.
- All four gaps in what the test covers matter: team match counts, player statistics, how play unfolds, and season and career figures.
- The first version of the test closes three coverage gaps together: team match counts, player statistics per player and position, and how play unfolds. Why: The manager reads player totals on every screen, and play can be wrong while totals pass. Accepted risk: Player statistics and the flow of play rest on a few hundred free event and tracking matches, mostly from tournaments and a few clubs, so their targets are thin and may not describe an ordinary league season.
- Season and career figures wait longest; until they arrive, people watching seasons at the milestones cover them. Why: The season layer comes last in the build order. Accepted risk: Drift over long saves goes unmeasured until the season figures arrive.

### Judging one change (session 11)
- A change run compares the new engine with the old engine on paired seeds, to show what the change moved even inside a band, and with the real target, which decides pass or fail. Why: A move inside a margin is invisible to the real target alone, and drift from the target is invisible to the old engine alone.
- Every change run plays the same fixed number of paired matches; milestone and release runs keep early stopping. Why: One count is easier to read and compare between runs. Accepted risk: Clear changes spend the whole budget, and subtle changes come back not sure yet more often, so the developer decides more calls by hand.
- The agreement between paired matches is measured now on today's engine with the flag comparison, and again after the random streams split, before the change-run count is fixed. Why: The whole per-change budget depends on that number, and the two measures show what the split buys.
- A change verdict on every band arrives within ten minutes; a change run plays at least 12,000 matches in total (6,000 pairs), and more when they fit. Why: A large floor keeps subtle changes visible, and ten minutes is still short enough to tune by. Accepted risk: At today's speed the floor takes about 16 minutes; the harness needs about 1.6 times today's speed to meet the floor and about 12 times to fill ten minutes with about 90,000 matches. This replaced: a verdict on the touched bands within about five minutes, from session 10.
- Before a change run, the developer states which bands the change should move and in which direction; the report flags every other band that moved and every expected band that did not move or moved the wrong way. Why: Hundreds of bands move by chance on every run, and a stated intent keeps the report short.
- The sensitivity family becomes a library of stated rules checked at several levels of a setting, and each change runs the rules for the settings it touches. Why: A broken rule is found at the change that broke it. Accepted risk: The rules share the change budget, so fewer matches remain for the bands.
- An unexpected move that stays inside the real margin does not block; the developer writes one line in the stored report saying whether it is a real side effect or a bug before the change goes in. Why: A surprise inside the margin is not a failure, but it must be explained.
- A surprise the developer explains as a real side effect becomes a new sensitivity rule (for example, harder pressing raises fouls), so the next change expects it. Why: An explained side effect should be expected next time.
- When a change pushes a band out of its margin, the developer fixes or reverts the change, and does not move another setting only to push that band back, because that chases the band instead of the cause. Why: Pushing a band back with another setting treats the band as a target, not the cause.

### The pass rule (session 11)
- A sound engine must pass at least 97 percent of change runs, so each band's margin and match count are sized for the band to pass a sound engine about 99.99 percent of the time. Why: With hundreds of bands, small per-band chances of a false fail add up, and a test that fails often gets ignored.
- Rare-event bands, such as red cards, penalties, and own goals, are judged in every change run with a wider margin that catches only large errors, and judged again with the normal margin in milestone and release runs. Why: Every change run still judges every band. Accepted risk: A small error in a rare band passes change runs and shows only at the next milestone.
- Each band's margin is worked out on its own: the widest margin that still catches the smallest error a player would notice, checked against the 97 percent target, with a stated reason per band. Why: One share for every band is too loose for some bands and too tight for others. Accepted risk: More work up front: every band needs a stated noticeable error and a reason. This replaced: each margin as a fixed share of the real spread, for example half.
- A band with a design target passes around that target and must also stay within a stated distance of the real value. Why: A departure on purpose must not grow unseen.
- Each band's noticeable error starts from how much real leagues differ on that statistic, and watched reviews tighten a band later. Why: It gives every band a margin at once and refines it from what testers notice.
- The first version of the new harness uses the full pass rule from the start: an equivalence test, a margin per band, and the 97 percent target; fixed ranges are not kept. Why: It ends the conflict between fixed ranges and the new rule. Accepted risk: The first version of the harness arrives later. This replaced: the first version keeping today's fixed ranges, from session 10.

### Where the bands live (session 11)
- Every band lives in one registry with its target, margin, reason, and data source; the harness, the tests, and the module cards point to bands by name. Why: One place to change a threshold ends the two sources of truth.
- The slow tests keep their scenarios but take their limits from the registry and use the new pass rule, as part of the change run. Why: One truth for every limit.
- Every band is shown over versions, and a shift-detection method flags a drift to a person, who traces it to the changes; nothing blocks automatically. Why: Changes that each pass can together move a band to its edge, and a simple alert per run raises too many false alerts.
- A module swapped in configuration is a change like any other: a full change run on every band with a stated intent. Why: Every band can block, and a swap can move bands its card does not name.

### Testing every tactical setting (session 11)
- Test every setting through a covering array: a set of team tactics in which every pair of setting values appears at least once (about 100 to 150 tactics today), each played against a fixed field, and grow it to three-way pairs before release. Why: Testing every combination is impossible, and most faults come from one or two settings together.
- Each tested setting must pass four things: the match finishes with valid events, the realism bands stay in range for a team using it, the setting moves what it says it moves (its sensitivity rule), and it wins no more than its fit and squad allow. Why: A tactic can finish cleanly and still break realism, do nothing, or win too much.
- Beside the covering array, a search looks for the worst case: an optimiser that tries to find the setting with the highest points against the field, so an exploit is hunted rather than hoped absent. Why: A covering array finds broken combinations but does not look hard for the best one.
- The pairwise covering array of tactics runs at every milestone, a small slice of it runs in each change run, and the three-way array and the exploit search run before each release. Why: Pairs catch most faults cheaply, and the costly checks fit the release run. Accepted risk: A fault that needs three settings together shows only before release.
- The covering array covers both teams' settings together, so every pair of setting values across the two teams meets at least once. Why: It gives the widest net for combinations that break only against a certain opponent.
- Each tested tactic plays with a few squad types, for example fast, strong, and technical. Why: It shows fit and tactic together. Accepted risk: It about triples the matches.
- Every tactic setting joins the covering array: formation, mentality, instructions, both shapes, about 40 roles with duties, and player and opposition instructions. Why: No setting stays untested.
- The tactics arrays run as decided, with no cut: the full pairwise array at milestones and the full three-way array before release, possibly on a small low-cost Proxmox cluster plus a VPS. Why: No tactic setting stays untested. Accepted risk: The three-way array takes about 71 hours even at twelve times today's speed, so a release run lasts days, not one night, unless more machines are added.

### Realism beyond averages (session 11)
- The test compares whole distributions as shapes, not only averages: pass counts, possession chain lengths, shot distances, and similar counts. Why: Averages can be right while play is wrong.
- The test scores movement against tracking data as the Waymo challenge does: how likely real speeds, accelerations, distances to others, and line heights are under the engine, summed into one score per version. Why: Movement is what a watcher sees first, and no total measures it.
- Blind clip reviews and a real-squad back-test are not part of the test; the watched reviews at milestones stay. Why: The person chose distributions and the movement score only.
- The movement score starts on the free tracking matches (Metrica, seven German league matches, twenty A-League broadcast matches), and licensed league tracking is bought before release. Why: It scores movement now and grounds it properly before release. Accepted risk: Licensed tracking costs money, and the movement targets change when it arrives.
- Each whole distribution is compared at a few fixed percentiles, such as the 10th, 50th, and 90th, each a band in the registry under the same pass rule. Why: It fits the registry and the 97 percent target. Accepted risk: A shape that is wrong between the chosen points can pass.
- The round-robin and the tactics arrays rate each tactic against the whole field with Nash averaging, beside the clear-weakness rule, and flag a tactic whose field rating is far above its win rate. Why: A plain win rate can hide a cycle or a tactic that beats everything a little.

### Tuning (session 11)
- Fit every value that has its own data bottom-up to that data; let the optimiser and the emulator search only the values with no data of their own, against a training set of bands, and judge every result on held-out bands that no search may read. Why: Only top-down search turns bands into targets, and held-out bands show whether tuning found football or a trick. Accepted risk: Some bands must be set aside and never read by any search.
- Each tuning run holds out a fresh set of bands that its search may not read. Why: Every band helps some searches. Accepted risk: Over many runs the searches see every band, so the hold-out weakens as a test of whether tuning found football.
- The optimiser searches the values with no data of their own directly on the full engine, as chess engines tune; the emulator does not propose settings. Why: No emulator stands between the search and the truth. Accepted risk: Every trial costs real matches, so a search takes days and competes with the tactics arrays for the cluster.
- Tuning loss is zero inside each band's margin and grows with the distance outside it, summed per family with declared family weights; hard rules and must-not-regress bands are limits, so a candidate that breaks one is rejected; a band with a real preferred centre, such as a design target, keeps a small pull towards it; when no candidate wins, several trade-off candidates are kept. Why: A summed distance chases band centres that have no meaning and lets a gain on one band buy a failure on another. This replaced: a search that minimised the summed distance of all training bands from their targets, from earlier in session 11.
- The engine emulator keeps two roles, a sensitivity screen and milestone triage, and no longer proposes settings. Why: The optimiser now searches on the full engine. This replaced: the emulator's third role as a tuning tool.
- Feedback comes in four steps: seconds for smoke matches and replays, ten minutes for a change run, a milestone run, and a release run that may take days. Why: Each step answers a different question at the cost it can afford.

### Performance for fast iteration (session 11)
- Beside the ten-minute change run, a larger realistic evaluation of the engine (for example every band plus a slice of the tactics array) finishes within about 15 minutes; how much slower realism may make a match, and how many machines are realistic, is not yet known. Why: Fast enough to evaluate the engine many times a day.
- Speed is explored in four families beside more machines: faster engine code, fewer matches for the same certainty, many matches in lockstep on the processor, and the setup. Why: The person wants algorithmic and setup gains, not only hardware.
- No GPU: the engine and the harness stay on the processor and machines, one engine and one truth. Why: A GPU engine would be a second, test-only engine that breaks identical replay on every machine.
- A full-trace replay check on fixed seeds becomes the gate for every speed change, and the four result-identical distance-check speed-ups go in first, before any realism work. Why: They halve a match's cost with no change to any result.
- The realism work runs on a staggered cadence schedule with random streams split per subsystem, under one total limit for the whole step of about 1.9 microseconds that features share, rather than a budget per feature. Why: Features can trade cost between themselves. Accepted risk: One costly feature can use up the room the later features need.
- The harness uses three noise-reduction methods: summed logged probabilities for cards, penalties, and injuries; the shot's on-target chance computed at the strike with short side-runs for its goal chance; and a correction from an in-play results model for results, scorelines, and points. Why: Together they cut the matches of the binding bands by about 60 to 90 percent. Accepted risk: The engine must log every chance draw and become cloneable or forkable from snapshots.
- The first-goal fork for the goalless share and sequential stopping are not taken for now. Why: The person chose the other three methods.
- The six platform maths calls move to a pure-Rust maths library, the script's 2 ms timer becomes a watchdog that marks a match invalid, and a cross-machine replay check is added now, with the speed-ups. Why: Identical replay on every machine is already a promise of the game, and the fixes cost under 1 percent.
- The harness runs in one process with a pull queue and no barriers, keeps running totals instead of per-match buffers and files, checks a sample of matches (about 1 in 16 plus every outlier), writes event files only for kept outliers, and caches the old engine's results by build, content, fixtures, and seed. Why: About a quarter of each match's time goes to work after the match, and the cache halves every change run. Accepted risk: A rare invalid state can pass in a match that is not checked.
- The test plays full matches only: no short segments branched from saved states and no scenario bank. Why: Full matches are simpler and free of the bias that segments bring. Accepted risk: Per-event bands cost more matches than segments would.
- The tactics arrays stay in full, with no predictive model choosing which cells to play. Why: The person keeps the earlier choice that every cell is played. Accepted risk: About 960,000 matches per milestone and a release run of days.
- How closely the fast result model tracks the engine per match is measured, and the fast model is used to cut full matches only where the tracking is strong. Why: Two studies disagree by a factor of about fifty on its value.
- The speed-ups and the harness work run first on the owned 9800X3D, and hardware is decided later from measured speed. Why: The measured speed-ups may make more hardware unnecessary for the change run and the evaluation.
- Stop each tactics-array cell as soon as its bands are clearly inside or clearly outside, since cells are independent: a sound cell stops at about a third of its matches. Why: Cells are independent, so a sound cell can stop at about a third of its matches.
- Shrink the tactics array by pairing roles only where they interact (within a unit and with neighbouring units) and by mirroring left and right slots, instead of pairing every role with every other. Why: Roles far apart on the pitch rarely interact, and left and right slots mirror each other.
- Give each tactics cell its own check depth: one match for validity, a few for the sensitivity rules, and the noise methods for its bands, instead of about 200 full matches each. Why: Validity needs one match and rules a few; only the bands need many, and the noise methods cut those.
- Compare the curve of results against strength gap by its few fitted parameters instead of a separate band per gap, so the per-gap comparison costs about the same as one band. Why: A separate band per gap multiplies the matches the binding result bands need.
- Let the optimiser use the same noise methods and shared seeds across candidates, and a two-evaluation step method such as SPSA, so each full-engine search step costs far fewer matches. Why: Each search step costs far fewer matches. Accepted risk: Accepted only if accuracy holds: the noise methods keep the same expected value, and every tuned result is still confirmed by a full evaluation.
- Speed and accuracy both matter: a speed method is taken only when it keeps the answer unbiased. Why: The person said both are important.
- There is no scenario bank for rare events; corners, free kicks, and penalties are measured from full matches. Why: The person did not agree to it. Accepted risk: Rare per-event bands may need more full matches than the result bands.
- Test related bands together, such as the percentiles of one distribution or a player statistic by position, as one joint equivalence test, so fewer independent bands share the 97 percent target. Why: Related bands move together and should not each pay for the same bad luck.
- Test all bands together with one simultaneous test (a max-t bootstrap over paired matches) that sets one threshold from how the bands actually move together, so correlated bands need less certainty each while every band is still reported on its own. Why: One threshold set from how bands actually move together keeps the 3 percent false-alarm limit with less certainty needed per band, while each band is still reported.
- Use the predictive model only for the release's three-way tactics array, keep the milestone pairwise array in full, and let the engine replay every uncertain cell, every cell near a band edge, and a random sample. Why: It brings a release from weeks to about one or two nights while only engine results decide a pass. Accepted risk: A broken three-way combination in a cell the predictor rates as safe and the random sample misses can reach a release.
- Each change run checks every touched sensitivity rule at every level of its setting. Why: A rule that breaks only at an extreme level is caught at the change that broke it. Accepted risk: More of the ten-minute budget goes to rules and less to the bands.

### The older report: run identity and reuse (session 11)
- Each fixture's key is a hash of what the fixture is: scenario version, world seed, the two clubs, home and away, both formations, and a repeat number; the engine seed is stored beside it, a run grows by adding repeats, and the old-engine cache joins on the key. Why: Today a larger run gives later pairings new seeds, so earlier matches cannot be kept or matched to the cache.
- Each match writes one compact row of unrounded measures (counts, numerators, and denominators), keyed by fixture, into a column file per worker, with no events and no commentary. Why: The max-t bootstrap resamples paired matches, a changed band is judged again from stored rows, and the rows are the emulator's training data. Accepted risk: A 12,000-match run keeps a few megabytes of rows beside the running totals.
- Each run records its full identity (program build with a hash of uncommitted changes, content, flags, random-number scheme, measure definitions, and registry version); runs are small work units with a ledger that resumes a stopped run without doubled matches; a changed band or margin is judged again from stored rows, and a changed program always plays new matches. Why: A cached or resumed result is safe only when its identity matches exactly.
- Each harness step proves itself with tests before the next step starts: a grown run keeps its earlier matches, a resumed run equals an uninterrupted run, the estimators pass hand-checked cases (zero events, a missing denominator), and every observation mode gives the same result digest; each run reports time and memory per stage (play, checks, commentary, writing, disk, totals). Why: The harness decides every change, so its own defects must be caught before they judge the engine.

### The older report: statistics fixes (session 11)
- The equivalence test adds the real value's own uncertainty to the engine's; each registry entry records the band's definition version, population, estimator, resampling unit, and minimum exposure, and marks its margin as a design choice. Why: A band built from a small real sample must be harder to pass, not easier, and the allowed error must not pose as a measured fact.
- Rare bands use exact count bounds, so zero events give an upper bound of about three divided by the match count; a rare band cannot pass before its minimum exposure; the error of a mean uses the sample spread and is never zero; the summed-probability method still applies where it exists. Why: Zero ten-goal matches in 200 still allows about 1.5 percent, three times the limit.
- Early stopping uses error spending over a fixed number of looks; development, promotion, and release each have their own seed set, the release set is never seen by tuning, and a tuning winner is confirmed on fresh seeds with a fixed match count. Why: A normal interval checked after every batch is not valid, and tuning on the same seeds learns those seeds.
- Each run spreads its matches over many independently generated leagues, the bootstrap resamples whole leagues as the outer unit, and season claims use independent saves. Why: Certainty about three leagues is not certainty about generated leagues in general, and the fixture key already carries the world seed, so this costs no extra matches.
- Against the old engine, each band gets one of four verdicts: better, worse, equivalent, or not sure, judged against the band's noticeable error; for a band with a range, better means closer to the real value; the old flag verdict is replaced, and a result that is not significant is never reported as equivalent. Why: Moved or not moved hides the direction, and the old verdict rewarded the band centre without any uncertainty.

### The older report: emulator discipline (session 11)
- Each emulator carries a record of its engine version, random-number scheme, training runs, settings and ranges, supported contexts, and validation results; it gives no answer outside those bounds, and a new mechanism or random-number scheme makes it advisory until bridge runs check it. Why: A model fitted on one engine version cannot predict a rewritten mechanism.
- The emulator is used only when it beats a simple regression and a random search on held-out groups (whole settings, generated leagues, and seed groups), and its training matches are tracked against the matches it saves; when it stops paying back, it is dropped. Why: A model that costs more matches than it saves slows the loop it was built to speed up.
- About 15 percent of each milestone run goes to cases the emulator did not flag, plus a random check of settings it ranked as unimportant; triage adds evidence and never removes a correctness check. Why: The emulator cannot see an effect it never saw in training. Accepted risk: The sentinels take about 15 percent of the milestone budget.

### The older report: conflicts settled (session 11)
- A running rule checker sees every tick of every match and keeps only small state, with no tick list; it must report exactly the same violations as today's checker on a fixed test set before it replaces that checker; full recording with events runs only for the sampled 1 in 16 and every outlier. Why: A rare rule break must not pass unseen in 15 of 16 matches. Accepted risk: The running checker adds work to every tick; if its cost is too high, it is measured and decided again.
- In-match tools and match preparation are judged from full matches only: game-state bands over natural matches with the tool or the preparation on and off, on paired seeds; the forks at a set state are cut. Why: Full matches only holds everywhere, so no test depends on branching from a saved state. Accepted risk: The effect of one change at one moment is much noisier than in a fork, so these checks need more matches or return not sure more often.
- Only tactics-array cells stop early, under the valid stopping rule; change runs, milestone runs, and release runs play their full count. Why: Full counts are simpler to read and compare. Accepted risk: Milestone and release runs spend more machine time on results that were already clear.

## Sketches

- The whole game sketched on the dark Broadcast Blue look, 70 screens: 40 first, then 15 more for match day, players and staff, and the season and world, then 15 more for tactics and training, the club, youth, loans and goalkeepers — https://claude.ai/artifact/5shzExrW9n9qWAVcPxD3Kr — idea

## Design decisions (session 9)

- The game uses the dark Broadcast Blue look as its main look; the product rules that ask for a light scene first and no dark panels change to match it. Chosen by you.
- The sketch goes deeper in three groups: match day (extra time, penalties, red cards, the other manager's changes), players and staff (all 32 manager attributes, every staff role, a full player profile), and the season and world (international duty with jet lag and injuries, transfer windows, the job market over years). Chosen by you.
- The decided staff list has 25 roles, plus the manager. The number 33 in the staff room is the count of people in post, not the count of roles. Found by the agent while drawing.

## Ideas still open

- Split the bands into a few dozen gate bands, judged under a strict family rule, that can block a change, and watch bands, judged under a false-discovery rule, that go to a person's dashboard. Raised by the agent.
- Fit a hierarchical model of real football (goals, shots, cards, timing, the table) on the five seasons of data, and compare the engine with its predicted spread for each statistic instead of ranges set by hand. Raised by the agent.
- Judge each change as the chess test farm does: paired seeds per match, a sequential test with fixed bounds per gate band, and a stop as soon as the verdict is clear. Raised by the agent.
- Use today's flag comparison to measure how strongly paired matches correlate before and after the stream split, because the whole per-change budget depends on that number. Raised by the agent.
- Give every band one implausibility score that combines sampling noise, data error, and a design allowance, and fail a run when any gate band scores above 3. Raised by the agent.
- Write the sensitivity family as a library of stated relations (raise finishing, goals must not fall; unrelated bands stay equivalent), each checked with a sweep across several levels instead of one before-and-after pair. Raised by the agent.
- Seventy screens sketch the whole game with every decision built, on the Broadcast Blue look; each screen lists the decisions it shows. Sketch: https://claude.ai/artifact/5shzExrW9n9qWAVcPxD3Kr (an idea, not a confirmed design). Raised by the agent.
- A positional tick-based engine runs the matches the player watches, and a lighter statistical model runs background fixtures. Raised by you.
- Per-match targets for both teams together: about 2.6 to 2.9 goals, 24 to 26 shots, 8 to 9 on target, 9 to 11 corners, 20 to 24 fouls, 3.5 to 4.5 yellow cards, a red card in about one match in eight to ten, and pass completion about 78 to 85 percent. Raised by you.
- Keepers carry an eccentricity, a hidden tendency that sets how often a keeper dribbles, comes far out, or tries the unusual. Raised by you.
- Shot outcomes follow an xG model from distance, angle, body part, shot type, defensive pressure, and the shooter's and keeper's abilities: penalties about 75 to 80 percent, most shots from outside the box under 5 percent, and about 10 percent overall. Raised by you.
- The club calendar and season-long squad availability are one piece, because fixture congestion and rotation matter only when fitness and suspensions carry between matches. Raised by the agent.
- New performance-related and personality-related attributes should be created beyond the 37 the schema was believed to carry at the time. Raised by you.
- The technical open questions of the event contract design document (serialisation, kind names on the wire, the Delete record, freeze-frame scope, the change-queue fields, the envelope version, and the keeper kinds) are questions for the plan of that build step, not for this board. Raised by the agent.
- Each band records the data set it was measured on, so a band can be measured again on another set later at low cost if the licence position ever changes. Raised by the agent.
- Agents follow the same rule as staff: a full person where an active league needs one, and a light record elsewhere, so ten thousand agents do not slow the save. Raised by the agent.
- Member ownership joins the owner types of the club business: members elect a president, candidates make promises about signings and the manager, and an election year changes what the board wants. Raised by the agent.
- Over a long save the rules of football change, as they do in real life, for example FIFA's 2027 transfer rules, foreign-player limits, and competition formats. Raised by the agent; you did not choose it when it was offered.

### The staff around the squad (session 8)
- Give real and generated teams their strength the same way: fit the same results model to a real season and to an engine season, and compare how results fall per gap in fitted strength, so no mapping from player attributes to strength is needed. Raised by the agent.
- Check that the engine's fitted strengths spread like the real ones: the best attack about twice the league average and the worst about 0.6 times, and the same skill and luck split in the table. Raised by the agent.
- Keep the fitted model at whole-match level, and judge timing inside the match (goals by quarter of an hour, the effect of the score, the effect of a red card) with measured ranges. Raised by the agent.
- Extend the yardstick model beyond attack, defence, and home advantage only where the engine can supply the same input: strength that changes through a season, strength from shots on target as well as goals, and a home advantage per club. Raised by the agent.
- Fit the parts that change slowly, such as the draw correction and how scoring spreads, on a longer history weighted towards recent seasons, and keep the five recent seasons for the parts that moved, such as home advantage after the empty-stadium season. Raised by the agent.

## What we found

### The realism test, the statistical model, and fast iteration (session 10)

- The harness holds sixteen bands in one content file, and each band passes when a point estimate falls inside a fixed range; the harness computes a standard error for every band but uses it only to label a change as noise, never to decide a pass. Source: content/realism-bands.json:1; crates/engine-cli/src/report/bands.rs:24; crates/engine-cli/src/report/mod.rs:196; crates/engine-cli/src/report/baseline.rs:19.
- The flag comparison already plays both arms on the same seeds, but it compares the two arms' totals separately with a fixed margin of 0.05, so it throws away the noise cancellation that paired seeds give. Source: crates/engine-cli/src/calibrate/mod.rs:89; crates/engine-cli/src/report/compare.rs:16; crates/engine-cli/src/report/compare.rs:100.
- Each match draws every random number from one ChaCha8 stream, so one added or removed draw shifts every later draw in the match. Source: crates/engine/src/rng.rs:5.
- A full-engine match costs about 448 ms on one core (about 2.3 matches a second); on the 8-core reference machine the harness plays about 12.6 matches a second, a full 1,000-match run of every suite takes about 81 minutes, and a targeted run of one pairing takes about 87 seconds. Source: .ai/workflows/football-manager-match-engine/05c-benchmark.md:78; .ai/workflows/football-manager-match-engine/05-implement-tuning-loop.md:113.
- Recent tuning swept 181 settings of the contest levers by hand at 200 matches each, and no setting passed every band; a tempo setting that fixed passes, throw-ins, and ball-in-play time broke the discipline bands and the stronger-team check, so it was held back. Source: git 7fd8f12 and 6baf0d9; crates/engine/tests/tempo_and_restarts.rs:449.
- Thirteen slow tests carry their own realism limits on 200 fixed seeds, separate from the bands file, so two sources of truth can drift apart. Source: crates/engine/tests/strength.rs:15; crates/engine/tests/discipline.rs:138; crates/engine/tests/tempo_and_restarts.rs:449.
- The code has no optimiser, no emulator, and no sensitivity tool; every candidate setting costs a full engine run read by a person. Source: crates/engine-cli/src/calibrate/mod.rs:1; content/tuning.json:1.
- The harness keeps each match's event log only for outliers and bands only team and match totals, so no band reads a player, a position, or a sequence of play. Source: crates/engine-cli/src/calibrate/mod.rs:596.
- At today's 12.6 matches a second on 8 cores, the 1.5 million matches of a full release run take about 33 hours; an overnight run of 12 hours needs about 35 matches a second, and the realism work will make each match slower. Source: arithmetic from engine-speed-measured and the check load of session 7.
- At 1,000 matches the standard error of mean goals is about 0.053 goals, so a quick run cannot see a change smaller than about 0.1 goals (about 4 percent), and the goalless share of 6.3 percent is known only to about plus or minus 1.5 points. Source: arithmetic from goals-per-match-poisson-distribution (sd 1.675).
- To detect a 5 percent change in goals per match with 90 percent power needs about 3,300 matches per arm without pairing; with paired seeds at a correlation of 0.6 to 0.8 it needs about 700 to 1,300 pairs, but the correlation on this engine is not measured. Source: https://select-statistics.co.uk/calculators/sample-size-calculator-two-means/ and https://arxiv.org/pdf/2512.24145. Not yet checked on this engine.
- Failing to find a difference is not evidence of sameness; an equivalence test passes only when the whole confidence interval sits inside a margin set in advance, for example a fraction of how much real leagues differ from each other. Source: https://www.emergentmind.com/topics/two-one-sided-test-tost-procedure and https://arxiv.org/pdf/2603.16213.
- Hundreds of bands each tested at 5 percent raise false alarms on almost every run; the standard answer is a strict family rule (Holm) for a few gate bands and a false-discovery rule (Benjamini-Hochberg) for the rest, which are shown to a person, not used to block. Source: https://metricgate.com/blogs/holm-vs-bonferroni-vs-bh/ and https://arxiv.org/pdf/1406.7117.
- The Stockfish chess project judges every engine change on its test farm with a sequential test on paired games from the same opening, stated in fixed effect bounds and scored per pair, and stops each test as soon as the answer is clear. Source: https://github.com/official-stockfish/fishtest/wiki/Fishtest-mathematics.
- Confidence sequences and e-values give a verdict that stays valid however often a developer looks at a running batch, which fits a run that stops early. Source: https://github.com/gostevehoward/confseq/ and https://stat.cmu.edu/~aramdas/talks/JHU24.pdf.
- Paired seeds cancel noise only while both engines consume the same draws for the same events; one extra draw desynchronises everything after it, which is why each player and each kind of action needs its own stream. Source: https://arxiv.org/html/2409.02086.
- History matching scores each output by its distance from the real value divided by the combined uncertainty of the emulator, the simulator's own noise, the data, and a model-discrepancy allowance, and rules a setting out when any score passes about 3; an HIV model cut its plausible space by a factor of about 10 to the 11th over six waves. Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/1405.4976 and https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5516248/.
- Mozilla's performance tracking, with a simple test per run across thousands of series, raised about 12.5 percent false alerts and missed about 6.8 percent of real regressions, so long-run tracking needs change-point methods and a person to triage. Source: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.18377.
- Independent Poisson goals predict too few draws, about 10 percent fewer than observed; the Dixon-Coles correction with rho about -0.13 moves weight to 0-0 and 1-1, and a bivariate Poisson with a shared term does the same. Source: https://opisthokonta.net/?cat=48 and http://www2.stat-athens.aueb.gr/~karlis/Bivariate%20Poisson%20Regression.pdf.
- The scoring rate rises through a match and depends on the score, as Dixon and Robinson's 1998 model of goal times shows, so a flat rate per match is not real. Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2103.07272.
- After a red card to the away team, the home team's scoring odds rise by about 69 percent and the away team's fall by about 42 percent. Source: https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s00181-017-1287-5.
- A 2025 study puts skill at about 40 percent and luck at about 60 percent of how far clubs over- or under-perform in a season, against an older estimate of about 26 percent randomness, so the table's spread has a measurable luck share. Source: https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1177/15270025251374620.
- A hierarchical model of attack and defence per team (Baio and Blangiardo 2010), fitted on real results, gives a predicted spread for any statistic, so the engine can be compared with that spread instead of a range set by hand; such checks are conservative and miss small errors at the league level. Source: https://gianluca.statistica.it/research/football/ and https://sites.stat.columbia.edu/gelman/research/unpublished/ppc_understand2.pdf.
- Copula models join shots for and shots against, and newer work joins shots, touches, and a hidden tactical state, which is the published form of a joint band. Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2002.01193.
- The Waymo self-driving challenge scores a simulator by how likely the real recorded movement is under the simulated distribution, feature by feature in three groups (movement, contact with others, and staying on the road), summed into one realism score. Source: https://ml4ad.github.io/files/papers2023/The%20Waymo%20Open%20Sim%20Agents%20Challenge.pdf.
- A classifier trained to tell real play from simulated play scores realism by its accuracy, with 50 percent as the target, but it can learn artefacts of the data instead of behaviour. Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2512.20773.
- Blind real-or-simulated tests use about 4 to 10 raters on tens of short clips with an agreement score; raters near 50 percent correct mean the two are indistinguishable. Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2510.05444 and https://arxiv.org/html/2603.06181v1.
- Nash averaging over a round-robin of strategies rates each strategy against the whole field and ignores copies, while a plain win rate or Elo hides rock-paper-scissors cycles. Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/1806.02643.
- Football Manager's public history shows corner and narrow-formation exploits and unreal crossing while season figures look plausible: averages can be right while play is wrong. Source: https://fmtweak.com/match-engines/ and https://steamcommunity.com/app/1263850/discussions/0/5446505912996665707/.
- Out of the Park Baseball checks itself by simulating thousands of seasons from real opening-day rosters and comparing the results with what happened. Source: https://www.mojotech.com/blog/opening-day/.
- Tracking and event data give distributions to compare: pass-network shape, team convex hull (about twice as large in attack as in defence), team length and width, pressing intensity, possession chain length, and speed and acceleration. Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/1311.0641 and https://skillcorner.com/articles/how-do-physical-outputs-change-throughout-the-world-cup.
- Morris screening finds which of p settings matter in about r times (p + 1) runs, for example about 510 runs for 50 settings, while Sobol indices cost about 100 times as much and are best kept for the few settings that survive. Source: https://rdrr.io/cran/sensitivity/man/morris.html.
- A Gaussian-process emulator weakens beyond a few tens of inputs, needs at least about 10 design points per input, and a heteroscedastic form predicts the spread as well as the mean; a Sobol design can grow in later waves. Source: https://www.asc.ohio-state.edu/statistics/comp_exp//jour.club/LoeppkySacksWelch_TNX2009.pdf and https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/hetGP/vignettes/hetGP_vignette.pdf.
- Chess engines tune hundreds of values at once: SPSA moves every value together along a random direction and reads the result of two short matches, and Texel tuning fits values to results by gradient. Source: https://github.com/official-stockfish/fishtest/wiki/Fishtest-mathematics and https://www.chessprogramming.org/Texel's_Tuning_Method.
- With many bands, the practical loss for one optimiser is the worst band's distance in units of its tolerance; true multi-objective search weakens past a few objectives. Source: https://archive.botorch.org/tutorials/multi_objective_bo.
- Changing one setting at a time cannot see two settings that act together and can stop at a false best; factorial and space-filling designs find main effects and interactions from the same runs. Source: https://bioprocesstools.com/blog/doe-vs-ofat/.
- Multi-fidelity methods spend most runs on a cheap model early and the expensive one late; here the ladder is the fast model, then shortened matches, then the full engine. Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2510.10984.

### Long-term integration

- Football Manager long saves drift: generated players shift in attributes over decades, big AI clubs leave high-potential youngsters without minutes, and AI clubs stop buying the stars they need; league reputation moves about one point a year, which damps drift but makes it sticky. Source: https://www.videogamer.com/features/these-annoying-bugs-are-ruining-long-term-football-m/; https://footballmanager.fandom.com/wiki/Reputation; https://community.sports-interactive.com/forums/topic/581909-league-reputation/.
- Minutes are a leading predictor of market value for players first seen under 21, but no public study sizes how much minutes at 18 to 21 raise later ability; under 30 percent of loanees are 21 or younger, and players who stay at their training club have better careers. Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2212.11041; https://football-observatory.com/Global-analysis-of-player-loans; https://www.efcfootball.com/en/news/efc-and-cies-study-highlights-career-benefits-of-young-players-remaining.
- Playing in Europe costs little in the league: Europa League clubs did better domestically than matched non-qualifiers over 16 seasons, and a Champions League midweek costs about 0.1 points in the next league match, while European clubs make about 94 starting changes a season against 71. Source: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC7739226/; https://www.skysports.com/football/news/11096/11502109/champions-league-and-europa-league-impact-on-premier-league-results; https://theanalyst.com/articles/playing-europe-premier-league-form-stats.
- Football results show no hot hand: after controlling for strength, a win streak is followed by slightly worse results, and repeating the last result is slightly less likely than chance. Sacking a manager in a slump mostly rides regression to the mean; Dutch clubs that kept the manager recovered faster. Source: https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/full/10.1080/00036846.2014.969830; https://ideas.repec.org/a/eee/ejores/v148y2003i2p233-246.html.
- Wage bills explain 80 to 90 percent of league position, and wages predict better than net transfer spend. Studies find competitive balance fell after UEFA's Financial Fair Play, because a cap tied to revenue locks in hierarchies built on past success; parachute payments soften the drop but skew the second tier. Source: https://www.open.edu/openlearn/money-management/management/business-studies/stefan-szymanski-on-the-business-football; https://shura.shu.ac.uk/22038/3/Plumley-UnintendedConsequencesOfFinancialFair(AM).pdf; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Premier_League_parachute_and_solidarity_payments.
- Since 1995, 47 percent of clubs promoted to the Premier League went straight back down and 60 percent within two years; all three promoted clubs fell in 2023-24 and 2024-25. Of 42 relegated clubs from 2009-10 to 2022-23, 40.5 percent returned within a year, 64.3 percent within two, and 31 percent never; parachute clubs were almost twice as likely to go up. Source: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yo-yo_club; https://theanalyst.com/articles/premier-league-promoted-sides-relegation-championship; https://theesk.org/2025/08/12/the-analysis-series-yo-yos-and-parachutes-premier-league-relegation-subsequent-performance-and-financial-impact-2010-2024/.
- Points per game is the team statistic that predicts next season's points worst; goal difference and expected-goal difference predict better. The table after about 12 rounds correlates about 0.8 with the final table, and the leader on 1 January won the title in 9 of 20 seasons. No clean football figure exists for the season-to-season points correlation; the NBA shows 0.64 and the NFL 0.33. Source: https://johnknightstats.substack.com/p/which-statistics-best-predict-future; http://eightyfivepoints.blogspot.com/2016/10/want-to-know-where-your-teams-gonna.html; https://harvardsportsanalysis.org/2018/01/how-much-correlation-between-seasons-is-there-in-each-major-north-american-sports-league/.
- Titles concentrate: in 2000 to 2010 the Premier League had 3 champions and the Bundesliga 5, while in 2010 to 2020 the Bundesliga had 2 (Bayern won 11 in a row) and Serie A 2 (Juventus won 9 in a row); Lyon won 7 in a row, Rosenborg 13, and Scotland has two clubs. Champions' share of available points in the big five rose from 69.9 percent (1999 to 2004) to 80.5 percent (2014 to 2019), and imbalance rose across 24 leagues. Source: https://football-observatory.com/More-and-more-points-won-by-champions; https://www.cies.ch/cies/news/news-detail/article/new-report-shows-decline-of-competitive-balance-in-european-football; winner lists (tally).

### Fatigue, injuries, and fixture congestion

- High-intensity running differs by role: wide midfielders about 3,140 metres a match, central midfielders 2,830, full-backs 2,610, attackers 2,340, centre-backs 1,830. Extra time runs about 10 percent below regulation for high-intensity running, speed, and distance, with accelerations and technique falling most. Source: https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/abs/10.1080/02640410802512775; https://scielo.org.za/scielo.php?script=sci_arttext&pid=S1015-51632018000100016; https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371%2Fjournal.pone.0110995.
- Fear of re-injury is common after a hamstring injury and goes with both re-injury and lower performance; after a severe hamstring injury, top speed and high-speed running took up to five weeks of matches to recover, and a mild quadriceps injury about a week. Source: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13223650/; https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12884885/.
- Treatment choices change time out: a partial meniscus removal returns in about 4 months against 6 for a repair, and a high ankle sprain 71 days after surgery against 39 without; surgery on some hamstring tears gives similar returns with fewer re-injuries. Days lost per injury type differ widely between clubs, lower where medical and performance staff communicate well and higher after head-staff turnover. Source: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40815849/; https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9679836/; https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11877254/; https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10660205/.
- Jet lag lasts about a day per time zone, worse eastward, and stayed raised four days after an 11-zone trip; away disadvantage grows with time zones crossed, but regular travel did not raise injuries. At about 43 degrees high-intensity running fell 26 percent while pass success rose; cooler northern clubs had more injuries and warmer southern clubs more ACL injuries; altitude favours the higher team. Source: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8279034/; https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3378691/; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22092416/.
- An irregular match schedule, not total volume, goes with severe muscle injury across 30,097 player-seasons in the top five leagues, and load spikes carry 5 to 7 times the injury rate; compensatory training keeps non-starters' fitness tests level but leaves them exposed to a spike when they play. Source: https://doi.org/10.1080/00913847.2026.2657258; https://www.researchgate.net/publication/331267557; https://doi.org/10.3390/medicina57070645.
- A full stop in the off-season costs 3 to 21 percent of aerobic capacity, against 1 to 4 percent for players who stay active, and two weeks off takes two to three weeks to rebuild. Teams average 30 preseason sessions (10 to 51); each 10 more go with 22 fewer days lost per 1000 hours in season and one point more match availability, a weak but real link. Training injuries spike in July. Source: https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s40279-020-01407-4; https://doi.org/10.1177/0363546519899359; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12453838/.
- Elite players fall ill about once every second season, with a median of 3 days out (58 percent respiratory, 38 percent stomach), peaking in winter; respiratory infections in the Bundesliga cost about 12 days each. No football data measures spread within a squad, so spread is a design choice. Source: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27034126/ (73 teams, 1.26 million player-days); https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13084848/.
- Premier League clubs used 30 to 34 players in 2023-24 and only five players played every minute; a title-winning Lille season had the lowest squad use and the fewest injuries. Football Manager players have complained of too many injuries in every version. Source: https://www.premierleague.com/en/news/4025786; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25216043/; https://community.sports-interactive.com/forums/topic/594593-football-manager26-injuries/.
- After an ACL injury, elite players return to matches after about 7.4 months, and only 59 to 65 percent play at the same level three years later, with lower minutes and output in the first two seasons back. Source: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27034129/; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29927499/.
- The acute-to-chronic workload ratio has no evidence as a way to manage injury risk, and the ratio creates statistical artefacts, so load should not be modelled as that ratio. Source: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32502973/.
- Recovery is not complete at 72 hours: hamstring force, physical performance (2 to 4 percent down), muscle damage markers, and soreness are still impaired, while hormonal and technical markers have recovered. Source: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29098658/ (77 studies).
- Congestion raises injury risk far more than it lowers running: one Champions League team saw 25.6 against 4.1 injuries per 1000 hours with two matches a week against one, with running unchanged; across 27 teams, 4 days' rest or fewer raised muscle injuries by 32 percent; and the last 15 minutes of the second match in a two-match cycle carried 3.1 times the risk. Congestion barely changed results, and a meta-analysis found no effect on total distance. Source: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20400751/; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23851296/; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26682867/; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33068272/.
- About 30 percent of injuries involve contact and 70 percent do not; foul play is involved in 40 percent of match ankle sprains; 61 percent of hamstring injuries happen while running or sprinting; and almost half of match hamstring injuries fall in the last 15 minutes of either half. Source: https://www.uefa.com/multimediafiles/download/uefaorg/medical/02/61/67/86/2616786_download.pdf; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23813486/; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36588400/.
- Re-injuries are 12 to 16 percent of injuries and keep players out longer; 18 percent of hamstring injuries are recurrences, 69 percent of those within two months of return. A previous injury is the strongest risk factor, about 2.7 times the risk of an injury the next season and 2 to 3 times for the same hamstring, groin, or knee injury. Source: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7146935/; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16855067/.
- Median days out by diagnosis: contusion 4, functional muscle 4 to 5, concussion 5, groin 8, ankle sprain 8, hamstring, quadriceps, and calf strains 13, knee MCL 16, lateral meniscus 36, and ACL 205. Muscle injuries are about a third of all injuries (hamstring 37 percent of them, adductor 23, quadriceps 19, calf 13); severity runs about 17 percent minimal, 23 mild, 41 moderate, and 18 severe. Source: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7146935/; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21335353/.
- Elite clubs see about 8 injuries per 1000 hours overall (about 21 to 27.5 in matches and 1.7 to 4.1 in training), about 2 injuries per player and 50 per squad each season, with about 86 percent of players available for a match; lower injury burden goes with more points. Incidence has fallen about 3 percent a season, while hamstring injuries rose from 12 to 24 percent of all injuries. Source: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19553225/; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23645832/; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33547038/; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36588400/.
- Per minute of live play, high-speed running barely changes late in a match (plus 1.1 percent from minutes 0-30 to 60-90) while ball-in-play time falls 26.8 percent; forwards are the exception at minus 12.3 percent, and substitutes run 21.0 metres of high-speed running per live minute against 15.4 for starters. Source: .scratch/out/FINDINGS.md §16.2a (DFL, 7 matches, 57 full-match outfielders).
- Every match starts every player fresh; a recovery rate exists in tuning but nothing reads it, and there is no carried fatigue, sharpness, or injury duration. No band checks injury or fatigue rates. Source: crates/engine/src/player.rs:204; crates/engine/src/data/tuning.rs:129; content/realism-bands.json.
- An injury today comes from two flat rolls scaled by injury resistance: the tackled player on a won tackle or foul, and every player once a minute. Fatigue does not change the chance, and an injury has no severity and no time out. Source: crates/engine/src/fatigue.rs:86; crates/engine/src/sim.rs:1009; content/tuning.json:83.
- Energy today is one value per player from 1.0 that drains with speed squared, scaled only by stamina; pressing costs nothing extra. Below 0.7 one multiplier lowers speed, acceleration, passing, finishing, decisions, and composure together, and only regulation half-time gives energy back. Source: crates/engine/src/fatigue.rs:45; crates/engine/src/fatigue.rs:78; crates/engine/src/fatigue.rs:131; content/tuning.json:158.

### Set pieces

- Football Manager's set-piece exploits came from AI marking that ignored unusual positions, such as a lurker outside the box or three attackers unmarked after a short long throw, and each fix was a patch to marking assignment. Source: https://community.sports-interactive.com/forums/topic/154901-103-quotcorner-exploitquot-thread/; https://steamcommunity.com/sharedfiles/filedetails/?id=1653318969.
- A set-piece coach can double a team's corner goals: Arsenal scored one corner goal per 32 corners before Jover and one per 16.4 after, with non-penalty set-piece goals rising from 6 to 15 to 20 a season; Brentford's rose from 12 to 18 a season while those conceded fell. Source: https://www.premierleague.com/en/news/4187343; http://archive.trainingground.guru/articles/brentford-looking-for-set-piece-coach-after-cuevas-move-to-chelsea.
- At the 2018 World Cup, zonal marking conceded a goal on 6.0 percent of corners and mixed marking on 3.7; in Premier League tracking, a four-zone system conceded more shots but worse ones and fewer goals than a two-zone system; about 80 percent of Premier League teams use mixed systems. Squad height does not predict set-piece goal share. Source: https://vuir.vu.edu.au/40479/1/Kubayi-2020-Cornerkicks.pdf; https://global-uploads.webflow.com/5f1af76ed86d6771ad48324b/606e51c17bf6c8ba83d69a01_LaurieShaw-CornerKicks-RPpaper.pdf; https://www.olbg.com/blogs/premier-leagues-tallest-teams.
- There are 37 to 44 throw-ins a match in the local data and about half are kept under pressure; a throw-in coach raised Liverpool's retention from 45 to 68 percent. Long throws into the Premier League box rose from 0.9 a match in 2020-21 to about 4 in 2025-26, with one long-throw goal every 12 matches. Source: https://www.thisisanfield.com/2019/09/expert-analysis-shows-the-huge-impact-of-liverpools-throw-in-coach-thomas-gronnemark/; https://theanalyst.com/articles/premier-league-teams-still-more-direct-2025-26; .scratch/out/ACTIONS.md §13.
- Penalties per match ran 0.23 to 0.47 across the top leagues before video review, which raised the rate in most leagues by about 13 percent. Conversion averaged about 76 percent in the top leagues before 2019 and about 82 percent in the Premier League since, after keepers' saves fell from 19 to 12 percent under the one-foot-on-the-line rule. Keepers dive to one side on about 95 percent of kicks; kicks go in 63 percent of the time when the keeper guesses right and 90 when wrong. Shoot-out kicks convert about 70 percent, and a kick whose miss eliminates the team about 44. Source: https://www.spreadex.com/sports/blog/features/var-impact-europe-big-5-premier-league/; https://theanalyst.com/articles/premier-league-penalties-like-free-goal; https://pricetheory.uchicago.edu/levitt/Papers/ChiapporiGrosecloseLevitt2002.pdf; https://sites.northwestern.edu/nusportsanalytics/2020/12/04/the-numbers-behind-penalty-shootout-in-world-cup/.
- Direct free kicks are scored 3.2 to 3.9 percent of the time now, down from 5 to 7 percent in earlier eras; deliveries into the box from free kicks lead to a shot 27.6 percent of the time and a goal 3.3 percent. Since 2019, attackers must stand at least 1 metre from a wall of three or more. Source: https://theanalyst.com/articles/whats-happened-to-goals-from-premier-league-free-kicks; .scratch/out/ACTIONS.md §13; https://www.scottishfa.co.uk/news/updates-to-laws-of-the-game-2019-20/.
- About 5 corners per team per match; crossed corners lead to a shot in the possession 38.5 percent of the time and a goal 4.1 percent, short corners 32.6 and 3.3; short corners are 11 to 18 percent. Team xG per corner spans 0.016 to 0.067, and whole-possession goals run about 4.2 per 100 corners. Source: https://theanalyst.com/articles/premier-league-corner-stats-inswingers-vs-outswingers-short-vs-crossed-kai-havertzs-defending; https://www.premierleague.com/en/news/4187343.
- Non-penalty set-piece goals were about 20 percent of Premier League goals in 2023-24 and 2024-25 and rose to 26.9 to 28.3 percent in 2025-26, a record, with corners alone at 17.5 percent; the previous ten-season high was 23.9 percent. Counting whole possessions after a restart and penalties, the local StatsBomb set gives 36 percent. Source: https://theanalyst.com/articles/premier-league-teams-still-more-direct-2025-26; https://theanalyst.com/articles/arsenal-tottenham-corners-record-premier-league-title-race-relegation-battle; .scratch/out/ACTIONS.md §13.
- The bands cover corners per team, throw-ins, and goal kicks only; the stored baseline measures 0.004 to 0.007 corners per team against 3.5 to 6.5, about 27 throw-ins against 35 to 55, and about 7 goal kicks against 12 to 22, because nothing blocks or deflects a shot yet. There is no band for set-piece goal share or penalties. The planned slices add real delays, blocks and deflections, and penalty conversion, and leave out headers and routines. Source: .ai/workflows/football-manager-match-engine/implement-evidence/realism-bands-v2/baseline-table.md; content/realism-bands.json:16; .ai/workflows/football-manager-match-engine/03-slice-keeper-and-shots.md:67.
- A foul in the area gives a penalty with no advantage; the nearest outfielder takes it with less aim noise; the keeper does not dive in open play. The shoot-out has a random 2-metre dive and a halved catch chance, tuned by hand to about 70 percent and marked as not calibrated. Source: crates/engine/src/rules/mod.rs:237; crates/engine/src/rules/mod.rs:1020.
- No aerial model exists: heading and jumping are read by nothing in the engine, a ball above reach height cannot be touched, and the keeper has only a larger reach and a flat catch chance, with no claim or punch and command of area used only to pick players. Source: crates/engine/src/sim.rs:953; content/attributes.json:9.
- At a corner or free kick everyone walks to the formation anchor, and opponents are only pushed back to the legal distance: there are no walls, no marking, no players sent into the box, and no players left up. Source: crates/engine/src/rules/restart.rs:215.
- The nearest outfield player takes every restart except goal kicks and kick-offs; a corner is always one lofted cross to the team-mate nearest the penalty spot; a throw-in is a short lofted pass of 3 to 15 metres with no long throws; and a direct free kick is played as open play, with no choice to shoot or cross. Source: crates/engine/src/rules/restart.rs:76; crates/engine/src/decision.rs:521; crates/engine/src/rules/mod.rs:607.

### In-game management

- Football Manager shouts act on morale, last about ten minutes, and depend on personality; FM26 launched without them because they did almost nothing, and players asked for them back. FM players complain both of meta tactics that exploit the engine and of changes that do nothing. In EA FC a switch of game plan takes a few seconds to take shape. Source: https://www.passion4fm.com/how-to-use-touchline-shouts-in-football-manager/; https://www.thesixthaxis.com/2025/12/09/football-manager-26-update-26-1-0-brings-back-shouts/; https://www.videogamer.com/features/these-two-hilariously-broken-football-manager-tactics-have-been-banned-and-heres-why/.
- In one Bundesliga team's 98 matches, a formation change raised the team's attacking output in the next ten minutes with an effect size of 0.52 and lowered the opponent's by about 0.3, with regression to the mean a likely part of it. Source: https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/psychology/articles/10.3389/fpsyg.2022.914915/full.
- Substitutes scored a record 13.3 percent of Premier League goals in 2023-24, and 39.4 percent of goals after minute 75; substitutes run more at high intensity than the players they replace; and trailing teams that made their subs by about 58, 73, and 79 minutes improved their result 42.3 percent of the time against 20.5. Source: https://www.premierleague.com/en/news/3788724; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24413912/; https://ideas.repec.org/a/bpj/jqsprt/v8y2012i1n11.html.
- Teams trailing by one took 53.4 percent of shots against 46.6 when leading, about 3.4 points above level, while the leading side still scored 52.9 percent of goals because its chances were better. Source: https://blogarchive.statsbomb.com/articles/soccer/score-effects/ (2013-14 Premier League).
- IFAB Law 3 allows five substitutes in three opportunities, with half-time and the breaks before and within extra time free, and one more substitute and opportunity in extra time where the competition allows it. Permanent concussion substitutes are an optional law since July 2024: the concussion substitute does not count against the limit and the opponent gains one extra substitute; the Premier League, the FA Cup, and the women's top English leagues use them, and UEFA club competitions did not. Since 2025-26 a keeper holding the ball more than 8 seconds concedes a corner. Source: https://www.theifab.com/laws/latest/the-players/; https://www.theifab.com/news/the-ifab-approves-permanent-concussion-substitutes-among-several-changes-to-the-l/; https://www.theifab.com/news/the-ifab-tackles-goalkeeper-time-wasting/.
- Nothing measures whether a change during a match moves shots or goals: the calibration suites compare fixed setups, the flag comparison judges a feature flag, and the queue test checks only that a change applies. Source: crates/engine-cli/src/calibrate/mod.rs:46; crates/engine-cli/src/report/compare.rs:1; crates/engine/tests/tactics_queue.rs:156.
- The AI manager checks every 30 seconds and at once after an injury or a goal: it replaces injured players, tired players from minute 55, raises mentality and pressing when trailing from minute 70, and lowers mentality with time-wasting when leading from minute 80. It never changes formation or roles, and it uses the same queue, limits, and windows as the human. Source: crates/engine/src/ai.rs:175; crates/engine/src/ai.rs:259.
- The viewer plays at 1, 2, 4, or 8 times real speed, pauses its own playback, and pauses itself at half-time; the engine runs a bounded distance ahead of playback. There is no highlights-only mode. Source: web/playback.mjs:13; web/main.mjs:818; crates/engine-cli/src/serve.rs:339.
- During a match the manager sees nine team statistics, an energy bar and a word for each player, the substitutions left, and a chip for each queued change; there are no ratings and no assistant feedback. Source: web/stats.mjs:8; web/lineups.mjs:53; web/substitution-picker.mjs:13.
- Substitutions follow one rule pack: five substitutes in three windows with half-time free, and one more substitute and window in extra time. There are no concussion substitutes, and the pack path is fixed, so no competition can have its own rules. Source: content/rules/default.json; crates/engine/src/tactics/change.rs:389; crates/engine/src/data/mod.rs:35.
- A live change today is a patch of formation, mentality, six instruction levels, and each player's role and duty, queued and applied in one step at the next stoppage the rule pack allows (every kind except a penalty); a change with no allowing stoppage before full time expires. There are no per-player or opposition instructions and no shouts, and nothing ramps a change in. Source: crates/engine/src/tactics/mod.rs:75; crates/engine/src/tactics/change.rs:96; crates/engine/src/team.rs:186.

### Output and presentation

- An xG taken straight from the engine's true scoring chance would reveal the finisher's and the keeper's skill; a shown xG fitted on simulated outcomes from features a real provider has keeps the luck-against-skill question that makes xG useful. Source: docs/research/metrics-and-visualisations.md (researcher's recommendation).
- A catalogue of 180 items (144 statistics and model metrics, 36 visualisations) maps each to the live match screen, half-time, the post-match report, the data hub, opposition analysis, or the player profile, and marks which confuse casual players and which help tactical decisions. The most decision-useful are xG difference with xG per shot, field tilt with box entries by channel, PPDA with regain height, turnovers, transition xG with rest defence, line height and compactness, progressive actions, set-piece xG, fatigue drop-off, and runs received with the pass network; the most misleading alone are possession, pass completion, shots without xG, goals minus xG, raw defensive counts, save rate, and one all-in-one value number. Source: docs/research/metrics-and-visualisations.md.
- A cheap test of whether a person understands a system asks for a prediction or reason plus a confidence, and a what-if probe; open, non-leading questions scored against a planted cause by two coders suit the brief's playtest. Source: https://arxiv.org/abs/1812.04608; https://gamesuserresearch.com/games-user-research-methods/.
- A tactical overload can be found from positions: players per flank or channel per team, pitch control per zone, and where the chances came from. Having more defenders within 10 metres of the ball raised regains within 5 seconds from 30.2 to 36.2 percent. Source: https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10618-021-00763-7; https://www.sfu.ca/~tswartz/papers/pitch_control.pdf.
- Football Manager added match momentum, pass maps, and analyst requests to help players adjust tactics, and in 2025 players reported its figures as wrong, for example showing a formation they were not using; an explanation that contradicts the match does more harm than none. Source: https://www.footballmanager.com/the-byline/taking-attacking-insights-data-hub; https://steamcommunity.com/app/3551340/discussions/0/690866958234823370/.
- FIFA 12 recorded more than 10,000 new commentary lines and players still complained of repetition. Language models summarising play-by-play made 3,312 factual errors in 180 summaries, and structured input cut errors by about two thirds. Source: https://s204.q4cdn.com/701424631/files/doc_news/2011/06/1/588186.pdf; https://arxiv.org/pdf/2510.21034.
- An in-game win-probability model for football exists and is proposed for ranking the crucial moments of a match; Football Manager offers four highlight levels, key, extended, comprehensive, and full. Source: https://arxiv.org/abs/1906.05029; https://community.sports-interactive.com/sigames-manual/football-manager-2023/playing-a-match-r4736/.
- Action-value models credit defending unevenly: expected threat credits none, VAEP credits tackles and interceptions through the chance of conceding but not pressing without a touch, goals added credits interrupting actions, and on-ball value credits pressures directly. A ranking built this way agreed with scouts up to 21 points more than an older method. Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/1802.07127; https://www.hudl.com/blog/statsbomb-on-ball-value; https://www.americansocceranalysis.com/what-are-goals-added; https://arxiv.org/pdf/1802.04987.
- Public match ratings reward attacking actions most: shots on target carry the largest weight in WhoScored, SofaScore, and FotMob, clearances about half as much or less, and a defender in a dominant team loses rating for having little to do. The three systems do not agree with each other. Source: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40011215/ (Varley and others 2025, over 2,100 players).
- No assistant or staff analysis exists; the only reasons shown are commentary lines on the AI manager's changes. Source: crates/engine/src/commentary/templates.rs:157.
- The live panel counts from the engine's summary counters, while the reports count in the browser from the event list, so fouls, corners, and offsides come from two paths; no test checks one against the other. Source: crates/engine/src/observe/mod.rs:172; web/report.mjs:29; crates/engine/tests/full_match.rs:47.
- Positions of all 22 players are recorded on every tick, which is enough for heat maps and average positions, but no event names a passer and a receiver, so a pass network cannot be built. Source: crates/engine/src/record.rs:44; crates/protocol/src/event.rs:107.
- The live panel shows possession, shots, shots on target, xG, passes, pass accuracy, fouls, corners, and offsides; the reports add cards, restarts, and a list of goals and cards.
