- Home teams score 1.530 goals a match and away teams 1.179, a home advantage of 0.351 goals, and results split 45.3 percent home wins, 24.5 percent draws, and 30.2 percent away wins (1826 matches, five leagues, 2017/18). Source: .scratch/out/wyscout_rates.json (1826 matches, five leagues 2017/18).
- Per-match rates are tight across five different leagues: passes 839 to 888, pass accuracy 82.2 to 83.3 percent, shots 21.0 to 23.2, shot conversion 9.7 to 11.1 percent, duels 442 to 471, aerial duels 76 to 99, fouls 21 to 29, yellow cards 3.1 to 4.9, throw-ins 42 to 46, corners 9.5 to 10.3, goal kicks 16 to 17, free kicks 19 to 27, and offsides 4.0 to 5.0. Source: .scratch/out/wyscout_rates.json (1826 matches, five leagues 2017/18).
- Shot conversion halves roughly every 5 metres: 51.9 percent inside 6 metres, 21.6 percent at 6 to 11, 12.2 percent at 11 to 16.5, 6.3 percent at 16.5 to 22, 2.8 percent at 22 to 30, and 1.8 percent beyond 30, measured over 40461 shots. Source: .scratch/out/wyscout_rates.json (1826 matches, five leagues).
- In provider-labelled tracking over 14 halves, the ball sits above half a metre for 29.6 percent of live play, above one metre for 23.9 percent, and above two metres for 17.1 percent, and it is in play for 55.4 percent of the match, so a strictly two-dimensional engine is wrong about a sixth of the live game. Source: .scratch/out/idsse_ball.csv (14 ball halves, provider-labelled).
- Measured ball speed runs to a median of 6.47 metres per second, a ninety-ninth percentile of 24.9, and an extreme of 49.9, which confirms the earlier discard of a 57 metres per second reading as tracking noise and gives the engine a real speed ceiling in its place. Source: .scratch/out/idsse_ball.csv (14 ball halves, provider-labelled).
- The official DFL live schema carries 31 event kinds and 139 distinct attributes at 1423 events a match, against the engine's four kinds carrying no player, no coordinates, and no outcome; the earlier estimate that the contract change means about fifteen event kinds plus a player, coordinates, and an outcome understates the gap by half on kinds and by a factor of forty on fields (7 DFL matches, 9961 events). Source: .scratch/out/idsse_events.csv.gz (7 DFL matches, 9961 events).
- Whether a pass leaves the ground matters more than how far it travels: flat passes complete at 90.2 percent and high passes at 53.4, and a flat long pass completes at 68.6 percent while a high medium pass completes at 52.0, so the engine should branch on pass height before pass length. Source: .scratch/out/idsse_events.csv.gz (7 DFL matches, 9961 events).
- Only about a third of shots reach the goalkeeper, because 38.4 percent go wide and 22.6 percent are blocked by a defender first, so a shot model that resolves attacker against goalkeeper models the minority case. Source: .scratch/out/idsse_events.csv.gz (7 DFL matches, 9961 events).
- Data providers count the same action very differently: duels run at 64, 202, or 226 a match and through balls at 4.6, 15.6, or 44.1 depending on the provider, while providers that share a definition agree tightly, with carries of at least 5 metres at 287 against 273 (corrected from an earlier 271) and possession duration at 1.20 against 1.16 seconds; so every realism band must name the definition it copies. Source: .scratch/out/ACTIONS.md.
- The stream crate already serves a recorded fixture over the same protocol the viewer reads, at 50 ticks a second, so a real match converted into a fixture can play in the viewer beside a simulated one. Source: crates/stream/src/replay.rs:1 and crates/stream/src/replay.rs:14.
- The realism test has measured joint bands: goals are Poisson once the club is known, with a within-club variance-to-mean ratio of 0.99, but shots are over-dispersed at 1.64 and crosses at 3.03; one team's shots run from 6 to 17 between the 10th and 90th percentiles, shots and goals correlate at only 0.32, and home and away shot counts correlate at minus 0.34 (1826 Wyscout league matches). Source: .scratch/out/gap_wy.txt (1826 Wyscout league matches) and FINDINGS.md §16.
- Over about 20 seasons the league top scorer's goals run from 19 to 36 in the Premier League, 21 to 36 in Serie A, 16 to 41 in the Bundesliga, and 15 to 38 in Ligue 1, with medians of about 26 to 29, and LaLiga holds the outlier at Messi's 50 in 2011-12; the Premier League assist record is 21, and no source gives the tenth-best scorer's total. Source: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capocannoniere and https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Bundesliga_top_scorers_by_season and https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Premier_League_Golden_Boot (research sub-agent).
- The calibration sources carry different licences: the IDSSE DFL set and the Wyscout public set are CC-BY 4.0 and SkillCorner's open data is MIT, all usable commercially with attribution, while StatsBomb's open data is framed for research under a user agreement, Metrica's sample data has no formal licence, and the CC0 Transfermarkt dataset is scraped data that Transfermarkt's own terms and the EU database right may still cover. Source: https://github.com/spoho-datascience/idsse-data and https://figshare.com/collections/Soccer_match_event_dataset/4415000 and https://github.com/SkillCorner/opendata/blob/master/LICENSE and https://github.com/statsbomb/open-data (research sub-agent).

### The manager as a modelled entity

- Substitutions follow the score: across 1826 Wyscout league matches in 2017/18 a team makes 2.85 changes per match at median minutes 61, 72, and 82, a team that trails makes its first change at minute 57 against 66 for a team that leads, midfielders are half of the players taken off, and only 6.5 percent of changes happen at half time. Source: .scratch/out/gap_wy.txt (1826 Wyscout league matches) and FINDINGS.md §16.

### The season layer: competitions, careers, market, club business

- The board believed the match event carried only a team identifier, a score, and four event types (kick-off, goal, full-time, and tactics-change) and no player identifier. The code by the fifth session shows 17 event types and an optional player identifier, in the protocol crate's event definition. Source: crates/protocol/src/event.rs:16 and crates/protocol/src/event.rs:146 (17 event types and an optional player identifier, at session 5).
- Today's engine plays one match in 387 milliseconds of wall time on one core of an AMD Ryzen 7 9800X3D, so one 20-club league season of 380 matches takes about 147 seconds on one core and about 20 seconds on eight, before any realism addition makes a match more expensive. The match time is measured by the engine benchmark; the season figures are arithmetic. Source: .ai/workflows/football-manager-match-engine/bench-baseline/viewer-pitch/bench-1.stdout.txt:6 (bench.match_wall_ms 387; the season figures are arithmetic).
- The largest public career dataset is derived from Transfermarkt: 50,000 players, 65 competitions, 1.89 million appearances with minutes, 650,000 market valuations, and 175,000 transfers, released under CC0. It measures playing time, level, moves, and market judgement, and it carries no ratings. Source: https://github.com/dcaribou/transfermarkt-datasets (research sub-agent).
- Performance peaks at about 27.4 across 15,591 top-five-league player-seasons, at 26 to 27 for wingers and full-backs and at 27.5 to 28 for centre-backs and strikers. Wingers fall to half their peak by 31 and centre-backs by 35.2, and an 18-year-old in a top league plays at 50 to 85 percent of the eventual peak. Source: https://macro-football.com/other/aging/ (research sub-agent).
- Physical output has its own curves: across 5,203 GPS match performances speed peaks at 25.7, endurance at 24.8, and explosiveness at 26.0, and high-intensity output falls after 32 while endurance holds. Creative passing holds into the early thirties while shooting and tackling fade. Source: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12551122/ (abstract) and https://www.espn.com/soccer/story/_/id/37467220/ (research sub-agent).
- Of about 200 LaLiga academy players aged 13 to 18, 4 percent reached the top tier within ten years. Across 378 Bundesliga loans, loanees gained no measurable minutes and no measurable market value compared with 6,162 players who were not loaned. Source: https://www.essex.ac.uk/news/2024/06/27/just-four-per-cent-of-teenage-academy-players-make-it-to-top-tier-football and https://ideas.repec.org/p/zbw/umiodp/295740.html (research sub-agent).
- After an ACL rupture 80 percent of elite players return, after a mean of 216 days; they stay below matched controls for two seasons, with attackers declining further, 20 percent of professionals see their careers end within three seasons, and only 47.5 percent keep their playing level. Source: https://doi.org/10.1177/23259671211008892 and https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9859836/ (abstracts; research sub-agent).
- No public data records true attribute histories, training or coaching effects at scale, personality across a career, or the causal effect of a moment. The only attribute-style history is the FIFA 15 to 23 rating set, which is rated by people rather than measured. Source: https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/stefanoleone992/fifa-23-complete-player-dataset (research sub-agent).
- The revenue mix is measured: the top 20 clubs earn 43 percent from commercial income, 38 percent from broadcast, and 19 percent from matchday. The top ten lean on commercial income at 48 percent while clubs ranked 11 to 20 lean on broadcast at 49 percent, and the Premier League as a whole takes about half of its 6.8 billion pounds from broadcast (Deloitte Football Money League and Annual Review). Source: https://www.deloitte.com/uk/en/services/consulting-financial/analysis/deloitte-football-money-league.html and the Deloitte Annual Review pages (research sub-agent).
- In a selling league player sales are a main income source: across nine Eredivisie clubs in 2022/23 they were 29.5 percent of income on average and about 44 percent at Ajax and PSV. Source: https://offthepitch.com/a/player-sales-lifeline-dutch-football-financial-divide-widens (research sub-agent).
- Revenue spread inside a league is set largely by broadcast rules: Premier League central payments run 1.6 to 1 from bottom to top with about 2.7 million pounds of merit pay per place, Bundesliga TV money runs 2.7 to 1, two clubs take 52 percent of LaLiga revenue, and Eredivisie revenue runs about 10 to 1. Source: https://www.football365.com/news/2025-26-premier-league-prize-money-table-tv and https://bulinews.com/bundesliga-money-distribution-for-2025/26-season-how-much-club-get (research sub-agent).
- Wages run at 54 to 80 percent of revenue across the big-five leagues (Bundesliga 54, LaLiga 60, Premier League 65, Serie A 66, Ligue 1 80), and UEFA's squad-cost rule caps wages, transfer costs, and agent fees at 70 percent of revenue from 2025/26. Source: the Deloitte Annual Review pages and https://www.uefa.com/news-media/news/0274-14da0ce4535d-fa5b130ae9b6-1000--explainer-uefa-s-new-financial-sustainability-regulations/ (research sub-agent).
- Losses are normal and owners cover them: European top-division clubs lost 1.1 billion euros before tax in 2024, only 8 Premier League clubs made an operating profit, and Championship clubs lost 355 million pounds with one club in operating profit. Owner-funding totals are only a proxy built from club examples. Source: https://www.uefa.com/news-media/news/02a2-200452a66064-0cfd3f86b94f-1000--new-report-highlights-record-revenues-and-increasing-inv/ and https://theesk.org/2025/07/19/15922/ (research sub-agent).
- The richest league buys and the others sell: in summer 2025 Premier League clubs spent 3.0 billion pounds gross, about 44 percent of league revenue and 51 percent of all big-five gross spending, while the Bundesliga and Ligue 1 were net sellers by 180 and 305 million euros. Source: https://www.deloitte.com/uk/en/about/press-room/deloitte-premier-league-spending-record-eclipsed.html (research sub-agent).
- Prize money is published in full: the Champions League pays 18.62 million euros for the league phase, 2.1 million per win, and rising round payments up to 18.5 million for the final, from a 2.467 billion euro pot, against 565 million for the Europa League. Source: https://www.beinsports.com/en-us/soccer/uefa-champions-league/articles/uefa-confirms-prize-distribution-for-2025-26-champions-league-2025-08-27 (research sub-agent).
- Managers are sacked often and sacking does not work: the median top-flight tenure is about 243 days, 47 English managers were dismissed in 2014-15 and 51 in 2025-26, and across 331 mid-season changes in the top two divisions of five countries from 2017-18 to 2021-22, points collapsed before a dismissal while expected points stayed stable, and the new manager's effect against matched controls was indistinguishable from zero. Source: https://football-observatory.com/Coaches-average-tenure-duration-global-analysis and https://doi.org/10.1080/02640414.2026.2698238 (research sub-agent).
- A sacking follows results against expectation rather than underlying performance: a run of defeats, the relegation zone, and results worse than the pre-season bookmaker expectation all raise the risk, and of 78 Premier League clubs that sacked a manager in the relegation zone, 58 percent went down anyway. No study compares sacking rates by owner type or measures how fast a sacked manager finds work. Source: https://doi.org/10.3390/economies8040082 and https://www.skysports.com/football/news/11095/13218236 (research sub-agent).
- The CIES transfer-value model estimates fees from performance, age, position, contract length, and club, and its estimates correlate with fees paid at 76 to 86 percent. In 34 of 49 cases studied the fee sat within 2 million euros of the estimate, with a long tail of star overpayment such as Mbappe at 87 million euros over. Source: https://football-observatory.com/Transfer-window-analysis-over-and-under-paid (research sub-agent).
- UEFA has 55 member associations, 53 of which place clubs in European competition, with places set by a five-year coefficient. The three club competitions use a 36-club league phase since 2024-25, and the official 2025-26 access list defines which domestic place enters which round. Source: https://kassiesa.net/uefa/data/method5/crank2026.html and https://documents.uefa.com/r/Regulations-of-the-UEFA-Champions-League-2025/26/Annex-A-Access-List-for-the-2025/26-UEFA-Club-Competitions-Online (research sub-agent).
- League formats vary by country beyond a double round robin: championship and relegation splits (Belgium, Scotland, Austria), relegation play-offs (Germany, France), promotion play-offs (England, Spain), spring-to-autumn calendars in the Nordic leagues, different winter breaks, cups with or without extra time and two-legged ties, and a FIFA calendar that merges the September and October international windows from 2026. Source: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIFA_International_Match_Calendar and https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_football_league_system (research sub-agent).
- Historical league and cup results exist as public-domain JSON in openfootball on GitHub, as free CSV with unclear commercial terms at football-data.co.uk, and as CC BY-SA tables on Wikipedia; RSSSF is the deepest archive and offers no bulk export. Source: https://github.com/openfootball/football.json (research sub-agent).
- Real names reach football games through collective licences (FIFPRO for player image rights, plus league and club licences) or through community real-names files that the player imports into a game that ships fictional names. The import pattern has run for over a decade without a reported successful lawsuit, but no court has tested it, and a broken rights chain cost EA about 100 million dollars with Brazilian players. Source: https://www.fmscout.com/a-fm26-real-names-license-fix.html and https://www.sportcal.com/news/ea-sports-and-fifpro-in-long-term-licensing-deal-extension/ (research sub-agent).

### The open questions the three design documents left (42 in all)

- The attribute schema holds 36 attributes, nine in each of the four groups, so 14 slots are free. The board's earlier readings of 37 attributes and 13 free slots are out of date, and the first open question of the engine realism design document is answered. Source: content/attributes.json:1.
- The match event now carries 17 event types and an optional player identifier and second player identifier. The board's earlier readings (that the event carries only kick-off, goal, full-time, and tactics-change with no player identifier, and that throw-ins, corners, goal kicks, and offside do not exist) are out of date, so the event contract redesign starts from a larger contract than the board records. Source: crates/protocol/src/event.rs:16 and crates/protocol/src/event.rs:146.

### Preparing for the next match

- No public study measures the effect of opponent-specific preparation on its own; real home advantage, about 0.3 goals per match, is the nearest scale. Source: general knowledge, not yet checked.

### The staff around the squad (session 8)

- UEFA licences take about 60, 120, 180, and 360 hours from C to Pro; Goalkeeper A and Elite Youth A take 120; holders revalidate with 15 hours every three years. In 2017 Spain had 2,379 Pro holders, Germany 861, England 383. Source: uefa.com licence pages.
- Coaching research splits a coach's knowledge into professional, interpersonal, and intrapersonal kinds, which the UEFA Pro licence mirrors. Source: Côté and Gilbert 2009; UEFA.
- Scouts feel unable to judge potential before age 14 to 15, and judge by criteria mixed with gut feel. Source: survey of 125 Dutch scouts.
- Ten of twenty Premier League clubs had a set-piece coach before 2025-26, and Chelsea paid about 750,000 pounds in compensation for one. Source: Creative Set Plays.
- Top-flight clubs in England, Spain, and Italy employ about 11 medical and performance staff against 6.9 in Germany. Source: survey of 78 clubs.
- Poor communication between doctor and manager went with 82 against 88 percent match availability; better medical-performance communication cut hamstring days lost by about 4.1 per 1,000 hours per grade. Source: UEFA elite club injury study.
- A change of fitness coach or doctor went with more hamstring injury, and a new head coach bringing his own fitness staff went with about three times the burden that season. Source: UEFA elite club injury study.
- No study measures week-to-week consistency of staff work; the nearest evidence is decision fatigue and nurse workload, where each extra patient raised mortality about 7 percent. Source: JAMA Internal Medicine; JAMA 2002.
- Players disclose more to practitioners they trust, and doctors who report to coaches lose that trust. Source: sociology of sport and applied psychology studies.
- An English Category 1 academy must employ at least 18 full-time staff on at least 2.5 million pounds a year. Source: EPPP rules.
- Weak evidence says abnormal playing surfaces raise lower-limb injury about 2.6 times; natural-turf hardness is not proven to cause injury. Source: IntechOpen review chapter.

### How the player watches the match (session 8)

- The viewer today draws a flat top-down 2D pitch, plays at 1, 2, 4, or 8 times speed with its own pause, and shows a goal banner timed in real time. Source: web/playback.mjs:13, web/pitch.mjs:1, web/goal-moment.mjs:1.
- Football Manager 24 has five highlight modes (Key, Extended, Comprehensive, Full Match, Commentary Only), changeable mid-match, with separate speeds for play and highlights; FM26 adds a dynamic mode that shows more highlights as a tense match nears the end, and a 2D overview with data cards between highlights. Source: GGRecon guide; footballmanager.com FM26 features.
- Players complain that Extended takes about 20 minutes with dead time before highlights, and that the FM26 engine looks arcade-like; no source confirms a rewind, a post-match full replay, custom highlight rules, or build-up control. Source: SI bug tracker (snippet); AltChar review. Not fully checked.
- FM26 has seven cameras with adjustable height and zoom, an xG graph and momentum widget, automatic goal replays, and a pause on injury. Source: Football Manager Blog; SI dugout.

### Player traits, personality, and chemistry

- Top risk: stacked effects. Mood, personality, traits, and chemistry together could swamp ability, so the five-match test runs with every family switched on at once.
- The design documents still give traits a 1-to-100 scale and twelve entries; the board has thirteen personality attributes on 1 to 20 and about fifty traits. The documents change at the next reshape.

### Psychological factors

- In the engine today, only the AI manager reacts to the score, late in the match. Players do not read the score, and nothing models momentum or match importance.
- Top risks: double counting when behind, thin data for the finely cut bands, and a stacked mind family that may fail the tail band.
- Momentum and pressure fall under the soft combine and the tail band; the setback dip is the dip half of momentum; the crowd lift fits inside the home advantage.
- Composure acts on each action under a presser; pressure handling acts on how important the match is.

### Rules, officiating, and competition formats

- The engine already calls offside at the pass, plays advantage, gives second yellows, and runs extra time and shoot-outs. The card does not depend on how bad the foul was, and the referee has no strictness value.
- The engine's offside has no edge cases: interfering without touching, a defender's deliberate play, rebounds, and a defender off the pitch.
- The referee's tolerance and threshold now set the lines between foul grades; team instructions gain tackling; each competition's rule pack replaces the one fixed pack.

### Data for the new bands (session 8)

- Referees differ by about 1.5 times in yellow cards with teams removed (0.81 to 1.23 times the league rate, 7,203 matches).
- The video referee missed 17 clear errors in 2023-24 and 9 in 2024-25 in the Premier League, about 0.02 to 0.045 a match.
- Momentum evidence is weak: strength-adjusted studies find it small or absent, and no paper gives a next-goal rate with strength removed.
- Cup finals have about 20 percent fewer goals, home advantage disappears in derbies and halves in cups, and no card effect of derbies is measured.
- No study counts poor runs for top players; the tail band needs its own count from rating data whose licence is open.
- Rotation costs a little, less for rich squads, and more in congested weeks (16,720 matches, four leagues).

### Goalkeeper specifics

- The attribute file already has nine keeper attributes; positioning is shared with outfield players, distribution is kicking and throwing, and sweeping is rushing out.
- Keeper amplification stays at player level, so league bands must still pass; keeper eccentricity merges into the keeper traits.

### Environmental factors

- The engine today has no weather, pitch quality, or crowd; every match plays in the same conditions.
- Top risks: rain, wind, and pitch caps are guesses with no real source, and the parts of home advantage could add up past its band.
- Every part of home advantage shares the one measured band; the condition bands gain a windy band and a limit on distance from dry.

### Advanced analytical models

- Real choices weigh completion far above threat, so a threat model must earn its place in the comparison test.
- Top risks: threat may make choices less real, the cost may not fit the budget, and freeze-frame data is thin.
- The no-lever rule and the watch-independence rule now carry one named exception, this work group's stand-in.
- Public full tracking adds up to about 74 matches: PFF World Cup 2022 (64, terms not published), DFL IDSSE (7, CC-BY), and Metrica (3, no formal licence); SkillCorner adds 10 partial-visibility matches (MIT).
- Published models used similar amounts: 58, 20, and 45 matches.
- Public data fits pitch control, pass completion, receiver prediction, pressing, and the space part of the off-ball model; deep ghosting needs the PFF set.

### Technical robustness

- The engine already runs a fixed-timestep loop from a stored seed, and replays are byte-identical on one machine.
- The save-numbering gap is answered by versioned saves with migrations; old engine versions stay available; the data scan backs the rule that no calibration data ships.

### Match engine architecture

- The engine already has many pure-function parts, one central step, a three-hook plugin boundary, feature flags, and tuning in configuration; it has no module interface or registry, no modifier chain beyond fatigue, and one random stream in the code.
- Top risk: effects that cross module lines, such as fatigue, pressure, and decisions, may hide or double.
- The module refactor becomes the first piece of work; the families' soft combine is the modifiers' combining step; random streams split per module too.
- The engine is about 16,200 lines; fouls, offside, shot, fatigue, and steering maths are already pure, while the referee rules, possession, saves, and decisions write the match directly.
- One random stream feeds 31 draw sites, so a stream per module changes every match at once, breaks 12 draw-scripted test files and snapshot resume, and forces recalibration.
- Today's nearest seams are three plugin hooks, an unused flag framework, and configuration; one setting is read from an environment variable during a tick.
- The match-engine workflow is at the review of its lone-forward slice and not yet handed off.
- Independent Poisson goals at the league-average rates (home 1.530, away 1.179) predict 25.3 percent draws and 6.7 percent goalless matches against 24.5 percent real draws, so at league level the simple model is already close; the draw shortfall that the Dixon-Coles correction fixes appears only when matches are split by strength gap, because unequal teams draw less. Source: arithmetic from home-advantage-and-result-split.
- The yardstick has three open choices: which fitted model (plain team Poisson, a low-score correction, or a hierarchical model with shared uncertainty), how a strength rating is given to real and generated teams, and whether timing inside the match belongs in the model or in measured ranges. Source: talk turn, session 11.
- Five seasons of about twenty full divisions give roughly 38,000 matches, so a league-wide share such as the draw rate is known to about plus or minus 0.2 points; the noisy part is each team's strength, fitted from about 38 matches a season, and more seasons do not shrink that. Source: arithmetic: sqrt(0.245 x 0.755 / 38000) = 0.0022; per team-season sqrt(0.25 x 0.75 / 38) = 0.07.
- When every band must pass its own equivalence test, the chance of a false pass needs no correction, but the chance that a sound engine passes every band is the product of the per-band chances: at 300 bands, 99 percent per band gives about 5 percent, 99.9 percent gives about 74 percent, and 99.99 percent gives about 97 percent. Source: arithmetic: 0.99^300, 0.999^300, 0.9999^300 (intersection-union testing).
- At 12,000 matches the standard error of mean goals is about 0.015, so the uncertainty range is about plus or minus 0.025 goals; a margin of about 0.1 goals leaves wide room, while rare events such as red cards and penalties need far more matches for the same share. Source: arithmetic: 1.675 / sqrt(12000) = 0.0153; 1.645 x 0.0153 = 0.025.
- Today a team's tactic has 10 formations, 5 mentalities, five three-level instructions (pressing, width, tempo, line height, passing directness), and time wasting on or off, which gives about 24,300 team settings before roles and duties, and about 590 million pairings of two teams; the planned tactics layer (about 40 roles, two shapes, player and opposition instructions) makes it far larger. Source: content/tactics.json:1; crates/engine/src/tactics/mod.rs:23.
- Combinatorial testing studies at NIST found that most real software faults are triggered by one setting or by two settings together, and nearly all by six or fewer; a covering array puts every pair of setting values into at least one tested combination with a small number of runs. Source: NIST combinatorial testing (Kuhn, Kacker, Lei), https://csrc.nist.gov/projects/automated-combinatorial-testing-for-software.
- With about 40 role values per slot, a pairwise array needs at least about 1,600 tactic pairings; with three squad types and an assumed 200 matches each that is about 960,000 matches per milestone (about 21 hours today, about 1.8 hours at twelve times today's speed), and a three-way array is about 38 million matches (about 71 hours even at twelve times the speed). Source: arithmetic: 40 x 40 pairs, x 3 squad types, x 200 matches; 40^3 for three-way; 12.6 and 151 matches a second.
- An engine value can be set two ways: bottom-up, fitted to the data of its own mechanism (a pass-completion curve to real passes by length and pressure), or top-down, searched until the match totals hit the bands; only top-down tuning turns bands into targets. Source: talk turn, session 11; board rule realism-bands-are-outputs-not-targets.
- The engine steps at 50 ticks a second of match time (dt 0.02), about 285,850 ticks a match, at about 1.55 microseconds of processor time per tick, so one match costs about 444 ms on one core. Source: .ai/workflows/football-manager-match-engine/05c-benchmark.md:78; crates/engine/src/record.rs:391.
- On the Windows reference machine with 8 logical cores the harness plays about 12.6 matches a second, about 5.6 times one core, and writes one stats file per match. Source: .ai/workflows/football-manager-match-engine/05-implement-tuning-loop.md:113.
- At today's speed the 12,000-match change run needs about 1.6 times today's throughput to fit ten minutes, a milestone's pairwise tactics array (about 960,000 matches) takes about 21 hours, and the three-way array (about 38 million) about 35 days; every factor by which the realism work slows a match multiplies these. Source: arithmetic from engine-tick-cost-today, harness-scaling-today, and tactics-array-load.
- The cost of the realism additions per tick (timed scans for 22 players, scoring 53 actions, pitch control, off-ball claims) is not measured; if they make a match ten times slower, the change run alone needs about 16 times today's throughput. Source: arithmetic; no measurement exists.
- The reference machine is an AMD Ryzen 7 9800X3D with 8 cores and simultaneous multithreading switched off; turning it on may add about 10 to 25 percent throughput (estimated). Source: Win32_Processor reading; research sub-agent, session 11.
- The harness splits matches into 8 fixed shards, one per worker process, with no rebalancing; a shared queue could lift scaling from about 5.6 to about 7 to 7.5 times one core (estimated). Source: crates/engine-cli/src/calibrate/mod.rs:535; crates/engine-cli/src/calibrate/mod.rs:744.
- Each harness match allocates a fresh tick-record buffer of about 57 MB, validates it by re-reading it, and writes every event as JSON lines with commentary plus a stats file; the equal suite took 78.5 s for 1,000 matches against a pure-simulation ideal of about 56 s, so about 40 percent is per-match work outside the simulation. Source: crates/engine-cli/src/calibrate/worker.rs:238; crates/engine/src/record.rs:46.
- Stepping many matches in lockstep on the processor gives at best about 1.0 to 1.3 times for this branch-heavy engine (estimated), for a near-rewrite of about 16,500 lines into a second engine to keep identical; published batched simulators win mainly on accelerators, and MuJoCo MJX reports poor performance on branching code. Source: https://mujoco.readthedocs.io/en/stable/mjx.html; https://arxiv.org/pdf/2206.10558; crates/engine/src/sim.rs:847.
- The engine calls sin, cos, exp, and atan2, whose precision Rust documents as varying by platform and version, so identical replay on every machine is already at risk; moving them to a pure-Rust maths library fixes it. Source: https://doc.rust-lang.org/std/primitive.f64.html; crates/engine/src/decision.rs:731; crates/engine/src/shot.rs:65; crates/engine/src/sim.rs:472.
- Steering and overlap resolution loop over players as structs with an all-pairs check across 22 players, a candidate for per-field arrays that the compiler can vectorise within one match. Source: crates/engine/src/steering.rs:66.
- Steering takes about 865 ns of the 1.58 microsecond tick: separation checks all 462 player pairs and takes a square root before the radius test, and overlap resolution checks 231 more pairs. Source: .ai/workflows/football-manager-match-engine/05c-benchmark.md:103; crates/engine/src/steering.rs:33; crates/engine/src/steering.rs:88.
- Decisions run on every tick (decision interval 1): each tick computes anchors for 22 players, pressers, cover, and for the carrier about 120 segment distances, and several other scans cover all 22 players. Source: content/tuning.json:5; crates/engine/src/sim.rs:862; crates/engine/src/decision.rs:40; crates/engine/src/decision.rs:240.
- The tuning block (about 126 fields) is cloned every tick, whole player structs are copied inside loops, and the release build has no link-time optimisation and default code-generation units. Source: crates/engine/src/sim.rs:853; crates/engine/src/decision.rs:55; Cargo.toml.
- At today's 12.6 matches a second, 15 minutes holds about 11,300 matches; for 5,000 matches the cost per tick may rise to about 3.5 microseconds, and for 2,000 to about 8.8; a full-pitch pitch-control grid costs about 0.3 to 1 ms per evaluation, hundreds of times a tick, while evaluating only the 53 candidate points costs about 2 to 5 microseconds. Source: arithmetic; https://www.sfu.ca/~tswartz/papers/pitch_control.pdf.
- RoboCup's simulator runs actions every 100 ms and vision every 150 ms, so perception at 150 to 200 ms and carrier scoring every 5 ticks cut those costs 5 to 10 times, at the price of retuning results. Source: https://rcsoccersim.readthedocs.io/en/latest/soccerserver.html.
- Result-neutral speed-ups (link-time optimisation, one code-generation unit, no per-tick clone, a shared 22 by 22 distance matrix, squared-distance tests first) should give about 1.3 to 1.6 times, confirmed by the existing replay hashes; profile-guided optimisation adds about 5 to 15 percent; a spatial grid does not pay at 22 players; skipping dead-ball ticks changes results. Source: https://kobzol.github.io/rust/cargo/2023/07/28/rust-cargo-pgo.html; crates/engine/src/rules/mod.rs:548.
- Under the decided pass rule (99.99 percent power per band), a goals-per-match band needs about 8,600 matches at a margin of 0.10 goals and about 3,800 at 0.15; matches needed scale with the variance, so every percent of variance removed saves a percent of matches. Source: arithmetic: n = 30.7 x variance / margin squared, variance 2.806.
- Recording the probability the engine used at each chance draw and reporting their sum instead of the realised count keeps the same expected value and removes most of the noise of single draws: about 90 percent fewer matches for red cards, 60 to 75 percent for yellow cards, similar for penalties and injuries, and 30 to 55 percent for goals through the keeper-save and block draws (estimated); it works for rates, not for shares of matches. Source: crates/engine/src/sim.rs:1109; crates/engine/src/sim.rs:1072; crates/engine/src/rules/mod.rs:249.
- Paired seeds help a lot for changes that fire rarely (about 60 to 90 percent fewer matches) but little for settings used every tick, where play diverges within seconds even with split streams (correlation perhaps 0.05 to 0.3, so 5 to 30 percent fewer, estimated); today's comparison has no paired standard error and a fixed margin of 0.05. Source: crates/engine-cli/src/report/compare.rs:16; crates/engine/src/rng.rs:20.
- Correcting by team strength saves 20 to 40 percent on strength-suite bands and little on total goals; stopping early saves little per run because every band must conclude, but saves 70 to 90 percent on clearly bad candidates; xG is a weak correction because the engine's own mean xG is unknown. Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2003.03774; crates/engine/src/sim.rs:986.
- Most harness bands pass on the point estimate alone; a standard error exists but only the red-card checks use it. Source: crates/engine-cli/src/report/mod.rs:880; crates/engine-cli/src/report/mod.rs:938.
- One match alone took about 424 ms, but with eight at once each took about 573 ms, a 35 percent slowdown when every core is busy, the largest single loss. Source: research sub-agent measurement on the reference machine, session 11.
- After each match the harness validator checks every pair of the 22 players on every tick, about 66 million distance checks, over a 56 MB record buffer, and the event file flushes on every row; with one worker this adds about 137 ms to a 460 ms match. Source: crates/engine-cli/src/calibrate/worker.rs:236; crates/stream/src/events.rs:129.
- Link-time optimisation with one code-generation unit measured +0.9 percent and target-cpu native -0.4 percent, both inside the 3 percent noise, which contradicts the estimate of 0 to 10 percent. Source: .ai/workflows/football-manager-match-engine/05-implement-tuning-loop.md:122.
- Rented dedicated servers at auction cost about 6 euros per core a month, a two-core VPS about 43 euros a month, and a used or mini 8-core box about 30 to 50 dollars per core once; a pull queue keyed by fixture lets any machine play any fixture. Source: https://bex.co/blog/2026/09/17/hetzner-server-auction-dedicated-vs-cloud-fleet-nodes; https://docs.hetzner.com/general/infrastructure-and-availability/price-adjustment/.
- Before any other machine joins, identical replay needs the platform maths calls moved to a pure-Rust library, a hash test of fixed seeds on every operating system, and a fix for the 2 ms wall-clock backstop of the script sandbox, which can fire differently on a slower machine; no cross-machine test exists. Source: crates/script/src/sandbox.rs:5; crates/engine/src/sim.rs:472.
- The four speed studies together: harness trimming, turning on simultaneous multithreading, and a pull queue give about 1.3 to 1.6 times; result-neutral engine fixes give about 1.3 to 1.6 times; logged probability sums cut the matches a band needs by 30 to 90 percent; lockstep batching is not worth it; lower decision rates are the main guard against the realism cost. Source: four research sub-agents, session 11.
- Bands counted per event or possession (pass completion, duels, set pieces, pressing, shot quality, movement) can be judged from short segments branched from real engine states captured at stoppages, weighted by how often each state occurs and checked each milestone against full matches; about 60 to 70 percent of bands could leave full matches, with about 5 to 8 times fewer full-engine matches for them (estimated). Source: research sub-agent, session 11; crates/engine/src/snapshot.rs:75; crates/engine/src/snapshot.rs:235.
- Snapshots and resume already exist, but a restored snapshot restores the exact random-number position, so every branch replays identically; branching needs a small reseed-after-restore call, and restore resets the team timeline. Source: crates/engine/src/snapshot.rs:587; crates/engine/src/rng.rs:57; crates/engine/src/snapshot.rs:250.
- Google Research Football ships fixed academy scenarios such as corners and counter-attacks; a bank of about 60 set-piece, counter, and build-up starts harvested from real engine states, each replayed about 500 times, would cut those bands to about a tenth of today's cost (estimated); the Scene builder exists but only behind a test-only feature. Source: https://github.com/google-research/football/blob/master/gfootball/doc/scenarios.md; crates/engine/src/scenario.rs:26; crates/engine/src/lib.rs:23.
- Multi-fidelity Monte Carlo uses a cheap model as a correction that the full engine anchors, so a poor cheap model costs precision but never adds bias; with the fast result model and 30-minute matches correlating about 0.9, result and table bands may need about 3 to 5 times fewer full matches (estimated). Source: https://kiwi.oden.utexas.edu/papers/multi-fidelity-monte-carlo-peherstorfer-willcox-gunzburger.pdf.
- A surrogate fitted on 1 to 5 percent of the tactics array, with the engine replaying uncertain cells, cells near a band edge, and a 1 percent random audit, could cut about 960,000 matches per milestone to about 20,000 to 50,000, while only engine data decides a pass (estimated). Source: research sub-agent, session 11.
- Rare bands can branch from states where the rare precursor already happened, such as a red card before kick-off, which the harness already supports, for about 5 to 20 times fewer matches (estimated). Source: crates/engine-cli/src/calibrate/worker.rs:231.
- Stockfish tests every change in two stages: a short-time-control run on one core, and only for passing patches a run six times longer; tests stop as soon as the sequential test is decisive, simplifications use no-worse-than bounds (an equivalence test), and each worker's time control is scaled to its measured speed with a check that flags anomalous workers. Source: https://official-stockfish.github.io/docs/fishtest-wiki/Fishtest-Mathematics.html; https://github.com/official-stockfish/fishtest/wiki/Creating-my-first-test.
- Fishtest scores each opening's colour-swapped pair of games as one of five outcomes, which is about 15 percent more efficient than scoring games alone, and uses deliberately unbalanced openings to raise the signal per game. Source: https://github.com/glinscott/fishtest/issues/348; https://github.com/glinscott/fishtest/issues/865.
- Out of the Park Baseball quick-simulates seasons and adjusts league-total modifiers automatically until output matches targets within 2 percent, and Football Manager runs background matches on lower-detail engines. Source: https://seamheads.com/blog/2011/06/22/out-of-the-park-baseball-12-available-now/; https://x.com/WorkTheSpace/status/1755937575775195156.
- Factorio checks every integration test against a stored checksum, has a heavy mode that saves, reloads, and compares every tick, and checksums a cheap subset of state at run time; this is the practice for catching replay breaks before any statistical run. Source: https://www.factorio.com/blog/post/fff-62; https://wiki.factorio.com/Desynchronization.
- MongoDB detects performance shifts over the whole commit history with E-Divisive means (95.8 percent area under the curve), available as the open-source tool Hunter; Chromium re-runs a suspect range about 100 times before blaming a commit, and Google Benchmark lists pinning, fixed frequency, and medians to cut timing noise. Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2003.00584; https://arxiv.org/pdf/2301.03034; https://github.com/google/benchmark/blob/main/docs/reducing_variance.md.
- Eight engine benches at once run only 0 to 5 percent slower each, with all cores held at about 5.2 GHz, so the engine scales about 95 percent linearly; inside the harness the match itself costs about 492 ms at eight workers against 464 ms alone (about 6 percent, from the fresh 56 MB buffer and its page faults), and about 170 ms more per match (26 percent) is harness work after the match, mostly the all-pairs validator. Source: research sub-agent measurement, session 11; crates/engine-cli/src/bench.rs:125; crates/engine/src/validate.rs:113.
- Estimated harness throughput: 12.1 matches a second today; about 16.5 with a sampled validator, no record buffer, and outlier-only event files; about 17 with an in-process pull pool and no barriers; about 19 with simultaneous multithreading on; runs against a cached old engine count double; about 30 with Linux workers and the Mac mini; about 50 to 65 with one more 16-core desktop or a rented server. Source: research sub-agent, session 11.
- There are only six transcendental maths call sites; a pure-Rust maths library costs about 1.5 to 3 times per call but under 1 percent of a tick, and the script sandbox's operation budget is already the deterministic limit, so the 2 ms timer can become a watchdog that marks a match invalid instead of changing it. Source: crates/engine/src/sim.rs:472; crates/engine/src/observe/mod.rs:220; crates/script/src/sandbox.rs:1.
- The engine's 7 MB working set fits any cache, so the X3D chip gives no advantage; the best buy is a 16-core Ryzen 9950X desktop (about 1,000 euros, about 34 matches a second), or rented Hetzner servers for bursts (an AX52 at about 64 euros a month for about 15 matches a second); a shared-core VPS is worth using only as coordinator or cache. Source: https://www.hetzner.com/dedicated-rootserver/ax52/; https://www.techpowerup.com/338700/amd-ryzen-9-9950x-prices-slashed-by-a-third-over-msrp-drops-to-usd-434.
- A shot's on-target chance can be computed exactly at the strike from its three aim draws without changing play, and its goal chance estimated by about 16 short side-runs of about 50 ticks (about 7 percent of a match's cost), cutting the matches a goals band needs by about 54 to 77 percent; the simulation is not cloneable today, but snapshots could stand in. Source: crates/engine/src/decision.rs:571; crates/engine/src/shot.rs:22; crates/engine/src/sim.rs:487.
- Martingale control variates add, at each logged draw, the change in an in-play value model minus its expected change, which has mean exactly zero however poor the model; a toy simulation cut win share variance by 62 percent, draw share by 70 percent, and points by 61 percent, which unblocks the result bands that bind a full evaluation. Source: https://people.orie.cornell.edu/shane/pubs/AMP1.pdf; toy simulation, session 11.
- Forking a goal-suppressed copy of a match at its first goal and weighting it gives the goalless share about 70 percent fewer matches net of the extra cost (estimated). Source: toy simulation, session 11.
- A fast result model driven only by the fixture correlates weakly with a single engine match (squared correlation at most about 0.07), so multi-fidelity use of it cuts under 7 percent, and multilevel Monte Carlo is counterproductive; the fast model is better used as the value model inside the martingale control variates. Source: https://kiwi.oden.utexas.edu/papers/multi-fidelity-monte-carlo-peherstorfer-willcox-gunzburger.pdf.
- For rare changes, running both branches at a flagged site and forking only at the first difference makes a pair cost about 1.01 to 1.09 matches instead of 2; for every-tick changes forking saves nothing, and caching the old arm saves about half instead. Source: crates/engine/src/flags.rs:24.
- A truncated sequential equivalence test stops a sound band at about a third of the fixed count, but a run stops only at its slowest binding band, so milestone and release runs save about 10 to 45 percent, and clearly bad changes 70 to 90 percent. Source: toy simulation, session 11; Wald sequential probability ratio test.
- Under the best combination the binding bands are the result shares, needing about 4,300 to 7,700 matches, so a full evaluation takes about 3,000 to 7,500 match-equivalents, about 4 to 10 minutes at today's 12.6 matches a second, inside the 15-minute goal; splitting bands by strength gap multiplies this unless those margins widen, and pass counts gain nothing because decisions carry no chance draw. Source: arithmetic, session 11.
- A sampling profile of ten matches shows steering takes about 58 percent of the tick (about 915 of 1,601 ns), with separation, the speed clamp, and overlap resolution as the hot parts; decisions take about 12 percent; the ball has a carrier on only 19 percent of ticks. Source: scratchpad profile, session 11; crates/engine/src/steering.rs:66; crates/engine/src/steering.rs:33; crates/engine/src/decision.rs:40.
- Four result-identical changes (pairwise separation with a squared-distance pre-check, the same pre-check in overlap resolution and in the speed clamp, and an early exit in carrier options) took the tick from 1,601 to 727 ns and a match from about 450 to about 200 ms, with the full-trace replay hash identical on five seeds; eight processes went from 16.4 to 33 matches a second. Source: scratchpad profile copies (exp/crates/engine/src/steering.rs, math.rs), session 11.
- Removing the per-tick tuning clone measured 70 ns slower, and code layout alone moves results by about 30 ns, so changes under 30 ns need cycle counters to judge; this contradicts the earlier advice to remove the clone. Source: scratchpad profile, session 11.
- A cadence schedule keeps physics, movement, contact, rules, and fatigue every tick; carrier choice and off-ball anchors every 5 ticks with an immediate re-decision on a trigger; perception, marking claims, and pitch control every 10; off-ball run choice every 25; players staggered deterministically; it saves about 150 ns now and needs random streams split per subsystem. Source: research sub-agent, session 11.
- Every tick, the realism additions would cost about 3.8 microseconds (about 28 minutes for 10,000 matches); with the cadence schedule about 0.54; a proposed budget per feature (perception 80 ns, carrier scoring 200, pitch control 150, off-ball runs 100, marking 60, fatigue reserves 60, a shared distance matrix 80) keeps the whole tick at about 1.35 to 1.5 microseconds, 10,000 matches in about 9 to 10 minutes. Source: research sub-agent estimate, session 11.
- Expected times at about 33 matches a second (speed-ups without realism) and about 16 (with realism at the step budget): a change run of 6,000 new-engine matches takes about 3 and 6 minutes; a full evaluation of up to 7,500 match-equivalents about 4 and 8 minutes; a milestone's pairwise tactics array (about 960,000 matches) about 8 and 17 hours; and a release's three-way array (about 38 million) about 14 and 28 days. Source: arithmetic.
- Full matches run anyway for the result bands, which bind at about 4,300 to 7,700 matches, while per-event bands such as pass counts need only about 230; the same matches serve every per-event band, so playing full matches only costs almost nothing except for rare per-event bands such as set pieces. Source: arithmetic from full-evaluation-in-minutes.

### The older harness and emulator report, checked against the code (session 11)

- The formation suite numbers its matches as pairing times match count plus place, and that number feeds the seed, so a larger run gives later pairings new seeds and earlier matches do not nest. Source: the harness code.
- The share error is the square root of p(1-p)/n, so zero observed events gives zero uncertainty; the mean error returns zero below two values and uses the population spread. Source: the harness code.
- The baseline comparison treats the two runs as independent, although they share seeds, so it leaves the pairing gain unused and is not always the safe side. Source: the harness code.
- The flag comparison picks the arm that passes more bands and then the one nearer the band centres, with no sampling uncertainty. Source: the harness code.

## What we are assuming

### Still open

- FIFA's calendar has international windows in September, October, November, March, and June; clubs must release players for them; and FIFA pays clubs for World Cup players and compensates clubs for injuries on duty. This is the agent's recall, not yet checked.
- Known real rivalries arrive only with an imported database, because the game ships no real data. A world with generated names gets its rivalries from geography and events in the save.
- The crowd's effect on players' nerves and on late belief is a design value that must keep home advantage inside its measured range, because the public data measures the referee effect and the total but not the player effect.
- Bonus-driven play and contract-year effort are design values small enough that the measured bands still hold, for example the shooting threshold at the edge of the box.
- How much a divided squad plays worse is a design value, because no public match data measures dressing-room division. The value stays below the spread of skill between players.
- A squad can run only a limited number of set-piece routines properly, and the limit is a design value, because no public data measures it.
- The fast model can simulate several past seasons of every UEFA league inside a few minutes on the player's machine.
- A referee who leans towards the home side reads as realism rather than as the game cheating the player. Published research now measures that leaning (in fouls, cards, and added time) and ties it to the crowd, so the game copies a real effect rather than inventing one.
- Flight arcs, heading duels, and spin on every ball update fit inside the 10 percent CPU tripwire the engine build records. Tracking data puts the ball above 1 metre for 12 to 19 percent of frames, so the aerial path is not a rare branch.
- A realism budget measured against the shipped engine can be chosen before the additions are built, and the engine stays fast enough to run 1000 calibration matches inside it.
- The engine can model the duel as a first-class contested action at roughly 456 a match without breaching its performance budget.
- Each action family gets its own resolution model, with the action kind and the situation as the first inputs and the player's attributes and mood as the second, so no single family carries player quality for the rest.
- A hand-built choice model (the release mix by zone and pressure, a shooting threshold by distance, and a four-term receiver score with a pressure modifier) reproduces the measured shooting threshold at the edge of the box and the preference for the safe open receiver closely enough, without a learned model.
- A generated backstory produces a history that holds together: a club's past results, its partnerships, and its reputation agree with each other and with the squad on the books.
- The cost of changing the event contract after the viewer, commentary, and report parts of the engine build are built is acceptable, and it is smaller than the cost of disturbing a complete part of the build today; every consumer of the event stream being still unbuilt today is the reason to doubt it.
- Public data on real careers is enough to size age curves per attribute and the player-to-player variance around them, even though real data measures performance rather than attributes.
- A real age curve measured on performance converts to an attribute curve through the same deliberate amplification the game applies to attribute effects (made larger than their measured sizes so that a management game rewards a better player), so a player's decline reads on the pitch at the size real careers show.
- Moments are one source of the player-to-player variance that real career data measures around the age curves, not an extra spread on top of it, so the curves as a whole still match real careers.
- A result-only model can be calibrated to the full engine's results (scorelines, player totals, injuries, and development inputs such as minutes and moments) closely enough that a simulated past reads as the same football as the present.
- Training, coaching, and personality effects on development are set by design rather than by data, because no public data sizes them, and the career test bands only the factors the public research measures: playing time and level from the Transfermarkt career data, age peaks, physical curves, academy and loan outcomes, and ACL recovery.
- The spread of loan outcomes can be set by design, because the Bundesliga loan study measures only the average effect of a loan and not the spread around it.
- A valuation built from visible data only can be banded directly against the 650,000 Transfermarkt valuations in the public career dataset, because both read the same kind of visible inputs.
- Owner types and financial rules together reproduce the measured shape of club losses (most clubs losing money and owners covering it within the rules) without a band that forces the share of loss-making clubs.
- Real player and club names, likenesses, and badges can reach the game through a database the player imports, so the game itself ships no data that needs a licence.
- The result-only model can run the leagues of all 55 UEFA member associations at once without making each matchday wait, because only the leagues the player chose run the full engine.
- The team generator can produce believable competence ratings for every position without a scouting or history model behind them.
- The two sources of disagreement about a player's value, scouting noise and each club's own weighting, can be calibrated together, so the spread of transfer fees bands cleanly even though one valuation can be wrong for two reasons at once.
- Building the match-rules and tactics-and-ai slices against a player record that will change under them costs less than delaying both slices to change the record first.
- Consistency can be an attribute that narrows a player's match-to-match spread without adding team-level variance, so a player repeats while the team's goal distribution stays Poisson.
- The degree by which consistency narrows each action follows the order in which real players repeat (duels and physical output most, passing next, finishing and saving least), so the departure from the measured skill sizes is largest where the real data repeats most.
- A weaker-foot rating can give two-footed players a visible edge while real rarity and a small effect size keep two-footedness from dominating squad choices.
- A human can judge whether a simulated season reads as football from its table, its top scorers, and its transfer list, without watching any match inside it.
- Goal-scoring in the engine can reproduce a Poisson distribution of total goals while mood, personality, and player quality all move the scoring rate.
- Testing the physical bands (distance covered and time at each speed per outfield player, and the spread of peak sprint speed across a league) requires the engine to report per-player distance and speed, which the engine does not report today.
- Ball height costs the engine little, because crates/engine/src/ball.rs already carries a third axis and a third velocity component, so the work is in the rules rather than in the state.
- The realism test bands each action family against one named provider definition, StatsBomb for most on-ball and goalkeeper actions, SkillCorner for runs and pressing, and DFL tracking for carries, shielding, and goalkeeper positioning, rather than mixing definitions within a band.
- The deliberate amplification of player effects can be confined to player-level outcomes, so the league bands (goals, home advantage, and per-match rates), the Poisson scoreline, and the club-to-club spread band still pass while only the per-player residual band fails.
- One amplification size exists that is large enough for a manager to see a better player over about five matches and small enough for the best player's season totals to stay inside the real range.
- The realism test checks the style clash by fitting the measured possession rule (match possession equals 0.487 plus 0.949 times the difference in the two clubs' season means, plus 0.027 at home) across every simulated match with AI managers on both sides, and the fitted multiplier lands near 0.949; the person gave no preference on the test, so this follows from the decision that the rule is an output of the matches and not an input, rather than from a choice.
- Each StatsBomb definition has a DFL equivalent close enough to convert a band, and 7 DFL matches are enough to check the conversion even where they are too few to measure the band.
- DFL tracking at 25 frames a second converts into a fixture of 50-tick frames by interpolation, with player identities mapped to shirt numbers, and the viewer shows it without a change to the viewer.
- Adding more detail inside the planned model produces the emergent football that is missing when the numbers pass but the match still does not read like football.
- A manager-set trigger can live in the rule pack and the change queue, so no conditional tactic logic enters the engine's tick loop.
- Nine paired phases can be derived from the engine's own state rather than authored by the manager, so the tactic interface stays at two phases while the model runs nine.
- A team's block can be made to track the ball at the fixed measured ratio (about 0.63 metres up the pitch and 0.30 to 0.39 metres sideways per metre the ball moves) without explicit per-player marking logic in the tick loop.
- The AI manager's live decisions enter the match through the same change queue as the human's, so both sides change tactics the same way and no tactic logic runs inside the tick loop; this extends the assumption about manager-set triggers to every live change.
- A per-player transition default can be derived from the team's two transition choices and the player's role and attributes, so a manager who never opens the per-player screen still gets a coherent team transition.
- The engine can keep club identity, squad, and tactic as separate inputs to style, so the realism test can check a club-to-club spread band and still tell which layer produced the spread.
- AI managers that pick tactics from their four inputs (philosophy, opponent, match state, and season situation) produce, over a season, choices whose results reproduce the measured possession rule with its 0.949 multiplier, so the rule emerges as an output without being tuned directly.
- The three effects of the tactical attribute (information accuracy, learning speed for a new tactic, and the squad's mood and trust response) apply to AI managers as well as to the human, so both sides of a match are modelled the same way and only the source of the choices differs.
- A player of the game can tell mood-driven errors from an ignored instruction, because the squad's mood and trust are visible to the manager before and during the match.
- The other matches of a matchday fit in the background of a real-time match on the player's machine without slowing the player's own match or its view.
- A player of the game reads a habit that overrides an instruction under pressure as realism, because the match view or the report names the habit and the moment, and not as the game ignoring the manager. This is, in a narrower form, the same risk as the rejected bet that players would accept instructions landing weaker under a weak manager.

### Confirmed

- All four costs would hurt a player: waiting on matchdays, save creation time, save size, and memory. Confirmed.
- Team numbers today have three problems: nothing shows what they change, familiarity and partnerships may be counted twice (in each player and again for the team), and club identity is a word rather than a number the engine reads. Confirmed.
- The realism bands measured on StatsBomb open data can still calibrate a commercial game, because calibration uses derived statistics rather than shipping the data; StatsBomb's research framing makes this a question for StatsBomb, not a settled right. You confirmed it: you accept the risk.
- Whether a manager may keep a hurt player on the pitch is still unanswered; giving injuries a severity and a risk driven by play settles severity and risk, but not the manager's choice.
- Personality fits the existing 1-to-100 attribute scale and needs no new data type.
- A milestone review that a person performs by hand catches what the shape measures miss, and it happens early enough to catch a failed mechanism before the rest of the model is built on it.
- Real football publishes a distribution of scorelines and a team-to-team spread that the test can band against, the same way it published the three averages in the planned realism bands.
- The plans already in progress are the floor, so every idea on this board adds scope and none removes planned scope.
- The charter of the football-manager-match-engine workflow still describes that workflow correctly, although the product is now read as a management game and the charter records exclusions (leagues, seasons, competitions, transfers, and finances) that the product no longer honours.
- The manager is a modelled entity with attributes, and not only the AI module the shape document already names.

### Rejected

- The match engine ships first and the season layer builds on it, so no season requirement changes the engine's data contract after the fact. Rejected, because the decision to add the player identifier to the event contract when the season layer starts changes that contract after the fact on purpose.
- Per-player match ratings can be derived from the event stream the engine already emits. Rejected by a read of the code at the time, which found no player identifier on the match event.
- The realism programme still fits the earlier decision that every idea on this board belongs to one second workflow, separate from the match-engine build, although the full season layer (table, availability, calendar, and cups) makes that workflow larger than the match engine it follows. Rejected when the work was split into two programmes, engine realism and the season layer.
- One past season is enough history for a partnership to read as earned, so a pair with one season of shared minutes is distinguishable from a pair with none. Rejected when the save moved to several simulated past seasons.
- A transfer market and finances can be built on the attributes that the work to deepen the player model supplies, with no scouting model, no contract-negotiation model, and no separate player-value model.
- A duel resolved from attributes, mood, and ball state reproduces the real pass-completion curve by kind of pass (from 91.7 percent for a simple pass down to 30.7 percent for a cross) as an emergent result, so no probability table for each kind of pass is written by hand.
- Attribute effects sized to the spreads measured in real football, about plus or minus 7 points of pass completion and 5 of shot conversion between the best and worst players, still read to a manager as real differences between players.
- The three aggregate calibration bands (goals, shots, and possession) are a sufficient test of realism.
- The three calibration bands still hold once mood and personality move decisions; the extra variance does not push goals, shots, or possession outside them.
- Aggregate shape measures can detect whether mood and personality read as football, and no human watches a match as part of the test.
- The engine can carry a provider-grade event contract with a core of five fields (player, team, x, y, outcome) on every on-ball event, adding per-kind qualifiers only for the kinds the realism tests read, rather than reproducing all 139 attributes of the official DFL schema.
- Form, season context, and a club's reputation for late goals can be supplied as asserted numbers in the team file, with no season model behind them. Rejected: these inputs now come from the season layer.
- Partnership data for each pair of players can be generated for a squad of 22 without a shared history to derive it from. Rejected: the season layer instead generates a backstory of past seasons when a new save is created, so partnerships start from a shared history.
- Players of the game accept that their own instructions land weaker when their manager is weak at tactics, and read the loss of authority as realism rather than as the game ignoring them.

### Player traits, personality, and chemistry

- Real match data can measure how much heavy rotation costs a team, so the rotation band has a real target. Still open.
- Real data, for example match ratings over five seasons, can give how often top players have a run of very poor matches. Still open.

### Psychological factors

- Real event data gives how often the scoring team scores next within a set time, with strength removed. Still open.
- Real data shows a difference between big and ordinary matches large enough to band. Still open.

### Rules, officiating, and competition formats

- Public data gives card and foul rates per referee over whole seasons. Still open.
- Published incident reviews give how many clear errors the video referee misses per season. Still open.

### Data for the new bands (session 8)

- Confirmed: per-referee card spread, video-referee misses, and the cost of rotation have real sources.
- Partly: the next-goal rate must be computed from event data, and big-match differences are measured only for goals and home advantage.
- No source: the poor-run rate must be computed from rating data with an open licence.

### Goalkeeper specifics

- Public data gives goals prevented, rebound shots, and sweeper actions per keeper per season. Still open.

### Advanced analytical models

- Enough public tracking data exists to fit the in-play models and ghosting; so far the board has only thin tracking data. Still open.
- Tracking data: partly confirmed. Enough for all models except deep ghosting on clean licences; PFF's terms are not published.
- Tracking data: confirmed, about 74 full-tracking matches with PFF under the accepted risk.

## Tensions

### Still open

- The rule that every band is an output and never a tuning target pulls against the emulator's first role, a tuning tool that proposes settings toward the bands; once the emulator is trusted, the engine's confirmation can become a formality. Resolved: values with their own data are fitted bottom-up; the emulator and optimiser search only values with no data, against training bands, and held-out bands judge every result.
- The design documents state bands as fixed real ranges, while the board decided that tolerance is the real spread widened by sampling noise; the two rules disagree where one statistic appears in both. Resolved: the new pass rule applies from the first version, and fixed ranges are not kept.
- The release run of about 1.5 million matches pulls against an engine that plays about 12.6 matches a second on 8 cores today, about 33 hours, before the realism work slows each match.

### Resolved

- Comparing league totals only pulls against the three-layer league bands and the fast-model check per pairing of team strengths, which both compare by strength, and against a fitted results model, which predicts by team strength. Resolved: the yardstick compares per strength gap after all.
- Judging every band on every change takes about an hour, which pulls against the five-minute verdict budget decided earlier in the session. Resolved: both hold, and a harness performance project makes every band fit in about five minutes.
- The whole-game sketch uses a dark broadcast look, while the product rules ask for a light scene first and no dark theme for the panels in the first release. Resolved: keep dark; Broadcast Blue is the main look, and the product rules change to match it.
- Form feeding mood, and mood moving decisions, can make a winning streak breed more wins, while real results show no hot hand and a slight reversal after win streaks. Resolved: mood stays modest and banded, complacency after wins by personality, and opponents lift against a leader, so streaks stay at chance level (streaks-modest-complacency-lift).
- The decision that accumulated load raises injury risk pulls against the evidence that the acute-to-chronic workload ratio does not predict injuries and creates artefacts. Resolved: load is a model of incomplete recovery and fatigue sized from the congestion studies, never the workload ratio, and the congestion band checks its effect.
- Starting touchline instructions at once pulls against the earlier rule that every live change, tactical or a substitution, waits for the next stoppage for both managers. Resolved: substitutions and formation changes keep the stoppage gate; shouts and instructions start at once and spread through the players over time.
- The earlier decision that the human manager's tactical attribute sets how accurate the manager's information is, match analysis included, pulls against the decision that every statistic and visualisation is always true. Resolved: figures stay true for everyone, staff quality sets how much depth is available, and the manager's attribute acts on readings and estimates only.
- Letting a player's decisions and teamwork set how faithfully the player follows a choice instruction pulls against the rule that an instruction is always carried out as given. Resolved: the rule now covers positional limits only; the manager still never weakens an instruction, and a player's fidelity to a choice instruction comes from the player's own decisions and teamwork, which gives teamwork a job in the match.
- This area adds a picture, scans, off-ball and defensive utilities, claims, calls, and a 53-action option list for all 22 players, while the engine-structure area decided that every player thinks on every tick, 50 times a second. Resolved: players near the ball or in a duel think on every tick, the others at a human rate, and any event wakes a player at once.
- The checks chosen in session seven (a sensitivity batch per attribute, role, setting, and team number; a round-robin on every change; the setting grid, the conditions, and the exploit hunt before each release; per-league bands and staff seasons) need about 1.5 million matches for a release run, against a harness planned for a few thousand matches on each change. Resolved: about 1,000 matches on each change, split random streams for paired seeds, early stopping, touched parts at each milestone, and a full release run overnight on the Mac mini and the VPS.
- The tails of the contact draws and the named error events can count the same mistake twice inside one error band. Resolved: each source gets a fixed share of the band.
- Tactic familiarity sat on the club in session one, on the player in session seven, and among the team numbers too. Resolved: the player holds it, and the team value is derived.
- The season layer lets the owner type set style targets, while session seven lets identity set what the board and fans expect. Resolved: the expectation blends both, weighted per owner type.
- Finishing a skipped match with the fast model pulls against the rule that a result never depends on whether the match was watched, unless the fast model matches the full engine from every match state. Resolved: the game accepts a small gap and the check measures it.
- A budget on an ordinary laptop pulls against the rich world chosen this session: every staff member and agent as a person, every pair stored, every event of recent matches kept, and more draws on every tick. Resolved: no realism lever is pulled, and engineering optimises first when the budget fails.
- Identity acting only off the pitch pulls against the earlier decision that a club's style has three layers, identity, squad, and tactic, each adding its own part to how the club plays, and against the assumption that the engine keeps identity as a separate style input. Resolved: style on the pitch has two layers, and identity shapes them from outside.
- Team numbers acting on team actions pull against the familiarity family of the player's effective-ability layer, which already carries partnerships and tactic familiarity, so the same thing may count twice. Resolved: the player layer keeps position and tactic grasp; partnerships and cohesion act only on team actions.
- About 40 roles, many of them close to each other, pull against a sensitivity test that fails any role whose change moves nothing measurable: several close roles may fail, or pass only because the test tolerance is loose. Resolved: each role is tested against a neutral default, not against its neighbour.
- Storing attributes as whole numbers from 1 to 20 pulls against development: at one or two visible points a season, a player would sit still for whole seasons and age in jumps, which is why Football Manager is reported to keep hidden fractions. Resolved: the engine stores tenths behind the 1-to-20 display.
- Concentration now acts through the whole match and consistency now spreads actions within a match too, so both move a player's errors inside one match. Resolved: concentration is rare lapses and consistency is the everyday spread.
- Technique and agility both decide which hard skills a player can attempt, so an overhead kick or a diving header could read either attribute, and technique risks becoming a general better-at-everything number. Resolved: both thresholds per skill, a lean by skill type, and technique to try with agility to execute.
- Discipline follows each country's real rules, yet the real remedy for abuse in FIFA's rules and in Spain, Italy, and Portugal is a free exit with compensation, and the unions take such cases up. The chosen consequences of crossing the line leave out both the free exit and the union. Resolved: the tribunal can award any outcome up to a free exit with compensation, and the union appears only inside the dispute.
- Every staff role as a person, in every club of every UEFA league, means tens of thousands of staff to generate and simulate. That pulls against a save that starts in a few minutes and a fast model that runs most leagues with results only. Resolved: staff are full people only in the active leagues, and a light record elsewhere becomes full when it matters.
- Most realism bands are converted from 200 StatsBomb matches, and transfer fees and valuations are banded against the Transfermarkt-derived career dataset, but StatsBomb's open data is framed for research and the Transfermarkt set is exposed to Transfermarkt's own terms, so two of the test's largest sources may not be usable in a commercial game, while the event contract has already removed the same risk by using the game's own names with a mapping to outside schemas. Resolved: you accept the risk, and the bands keep their current sources.
- Making pitch wear and weather interact pulled against the planned realism test of three aggregate bands held over 1000 matches: either the bands must hold across every combination of conditions, or calibration fixes the conditions and so never tests them. Resolved: calibration groups runs by condition band (dry, wet, worn) and sets a band for each, so a condition is expected to move the numbers rather than fail the test.
- Real per-match rates vary by only about one percentage point in pass accuracy and two shots in shot volume across five leagues, while the planned bands were goals 2.4 to 3.2, shots 8 to 16 per team, and possession 35 to 65 percent, several times wider than the real spread, so the test as planned would pass an engine that is visibly wrong. Resolved: four measured families (per-match rates, the scoreline distribution and home advantage, pass completion as a curve by kind and length, and physical output) replace the three bands, and the assumption that the three bands would still hold is rejected with them.
- Banding physical output and making the duel the place where player quality becomes an outcome both need the engine to report per-player data, but the event contract carries no player identifier, and the event-contract change (already repriced as a full redesign) is deferred to the realism programme, so the chosen test could not run against the engine the board had agreed to ship. Resolved: the test reads a separate test-only output with per-player data, so it runs against the shipped engine and the deferral holds.
